Investigated why that one works and the food-name list didn't rather than assuming the fix was "just use SectionList too": that table has only 3 sections (Macronutrients/Vitamins/Minerals) and, by its own explicit comment, is deliberately never nested inside another ScrollView -- the food-name list has 70-300 headers AND is deliberately nested (`nestedScrollEnabled` is there for a real reason, gesture priority inside the builder screen's own outer ScrollView). Both differences point at the same native sticky-position-tracking code path as the risk, whether reached via FlatList's `stickyHeaderIndices` or SectionList's `stickySectionHeadersEnabled` underneath -- so reusing the working example directly was judged not safe to do blindly, and the person, offered the choice, asked for a fully custom approach instead of reusing (and risking) the same native mechanism.

Built without touching `stickyHeaderIndices`/`stickySectionHeadersEnabled` at all: `InlineSelectList.tsx` now tracks scroll position via FlatList's plain `onViewableItemsChanged` callback (a long-standing, ordinary JS-level API, not the crashed native mechanism) to find the topmost visible row, looks up which group (if any) that row belongs to, and renders a plain absolutely-positioned `View` over the top of the list showing that group's label -- a hand-rolled visual approximation of a sticky header rather than asking any native list primitive to manage real pinned positions. Required a small upstream change to make this correct: `lib/foodNameGrouping.ts`'s `GroupedFoodEntry` item variant gained an optional `groupLabel` field (set to the owning group's label for a real member, omitted for an ungrouped singleton) -- without it, a naive "remember the last header seen" scan would incorrectly keep attributing an ungrouped singleton that happens to sort right after a group block to that group, since Pass 3's flattened output interleaves headers/groups/singletons by sort order with no other signal distinguishing "still part of the group above" from "unrelated food that just landed nearby alphabetically." `InlineSelectOption` (`InlineSelectList.tsx`) and the `foodListOptions` mapping in `FoodLookup.tsx` both pass this through. The overlay itself hides whenever the topmost visible row is a real header (avoids showing a duplicate right when the real header is already visible) and whenever the topmost row belongs to no group at all. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on every touched file (clean). **Confirmed working on-device the same day** -- the person tested Dairy & Eggs specifically and reported it "works as I want it to," no crash, no ANR. Since this fix lives entirely in the shared `InlineSelectList.tsx`/`buildFoodNameGroups()` machinery rather than anything Dairy-specific, this same confirmation covers every category's food list, not just Dairy's own.

**Immediately asked to extend to "the rest of the lists" -- which turned out to already be covered code-wise (nothing category-specific about the sticky-header fix), but prompted a real, worthwhile full-category audit of the grouping algorithm itself, 2026-08-02, same day.** The grouping feature had only ever been tested against Dairy/Meat/Veg (the 3 categories checked when it first shipped) -- the other 16 (Alcohol, Algae, Baked, Bev, Brewing, Fats, Fish, Fruit, Grain, Herbs, Legume, Mixed, Mushroom, NutSeed, Sprouts, SupplementPowder, Sweets) had never been exercised against real data. Ran the same throwaway-script methodology (export real base_name lists, run the actual grouping logic, inspect the largest groups by hand) across all 16 before trusting them, and found the exact same failure shape found before, confirmed by reading real group membership rather than guessed: `GROUP_STOPWORDS` gained `salted` (combined Anchovy/Carp/Herring/Salmon/Trout in Fish and Cashew/Hazelnut/Macadamia/Peanut/Pistachio in NutSeed -- unrelated species/nuts sharing only a preservation state), `toasted` (combined Blueberry Muffins/Bagels/Egg Bread/Rye-Wheat-White Bread in Baked), `common` (combined Bream/Cuttlefish/Mussel/Shrimp in Fish -- a generic taxonomy qualifier, not a shared identity the way "Wild Blueberry" is), `sugared`/`unsweetened`/`peeled` (combined unrelated fruits in Fruit -- also caused several different fruits to collide on the identical stripped label "Canned, drained," a real display bug on top of the bad grouping), `mature` (combined Broad bean/Chickpea/Haricot bean/Kidney bean/Lentil in NutSeed -- ripeness state, not identity), `sugar-coated` (combined Almond/Chocolate/Hazelnut/Peanut in Sweets), `gluten-free` (combined Baguette/Bread crumbs/Buckwheat bread/Chocolate biscuits in Baked), `préemballée`/`préemballé` (French "prepackaged," combined wildly unrelated composite dishes in Mixed), and `dough` (combined Apple pie/Cheese-ham crescent/Cherry strudel via parenthetical mentions like "(yeast dough)," with badly garbled remaining labels once stripped, in Baked). Deliberately did NOT stopword `salt` or `powder` despite each also producing one bad group (Mixed's "X boiled with fat and salt" collisions; Herbs' mixed-purpose baking-powder/bouillon/curry/gravy collision) -- both words also anchor a real, coherent group elsewhere (Herbs' own "Salt" -- Rock/Sea/Table/Iodised salt -- and Brewing's own "Powder" -- Cocoa/Coffee/Chicory powder) that a global stopword would have broken, and there's no clean per-context way to tell the good and bad cases apart with the current word-level mechanism; left as known, narrower, accepted imperfections rather than risk breaking something that already works, matching the "reasonable v1, may surface its own edge case" standard this feature already shipped under. Re-verified every one of all 19 categories afterward (zero structural flags -- empty/near-empty stripped labels -- and a fresh top-group spot check) via the same throwaway-script method. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device beyond the Dairy & Eggs confirmation above** -- worth a look at a few of the larger/messier categories (Fish, Mixed, Baked) specifically, since those had the most real fixes this pass.
- **Redundant naming inside a group**: reported directly -- "I don't see a reason to have the header of 'Cheese' with all things... also named redundantly as (name) cheese, rather than just keeping the name." `lib/foodNameGrouping.ts` reworked so each candidate-key match (comma-prefix, last word, or first word) now also computes the name with that exact matched word/clause removed, and item entries display that stripped text ("Cheddar," "Brick," "Dry white, queso seco") while `value` -- what's actually used to resolve the food -- still carries the real, full, untouched base_name. Re-verified against real Dairy/Meat/Veg data (same throwaway-script method as the original grouping work) with an explicit scan for broken (empty/near-empty) strips across all three categories -- zero flagged. Every touched file verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device**, same caveat as the grouping feature itself.

**A real, serious root-cause fix to `split_prep_method()` itself, 2026-08-02, same day -- the systemic risk this file had only flagged as a "worth auditing later" item turned out to be actively destroying real product-identity data at scale.** Reported directly: "all of the brand names have been removed" from Alcohol, with the person explicitly wanting beers/wines/spirits to stay individually identifiable for selecting the correct product. Investigated rather than assumed: confirmed the underlying government nutrition sources (USDA, UK_CoFID, Germany_BLS, Australia_AFCD -- the 4 that contribute any alcohol data at all; Canada_CNF and France_Ciqual have none) essentially never carried brand data for wine or spirits to begin with -- every wine entry across all 4 sources is a grape varietal or wine style, every spirit a category+proof, matching how real national food-composition databases are actually built. The ONE place real brand data did exist -- 3 USDA rows, BUDWEISER/BUD LIGHT/BUDWEISER SELECT -- WAS being lost, and investigating why surfaced something much bigger: the same `split_prep_method()` mechanism already fixed narrowly twice this session (a dropped cheese variety, a dropped egg species) was silently collapsing entire real product families -- confirmed by direct query, not guessed. All 8 of USDA's real beer variants (regular, light, higher-alcohol, low-carb, and the 3 branded ones) had collapsed into one indistinguishable "Beer Alcoholic Beverage" with no prep_method to tell them apart either; all 36 of USDA's real wine varietals (Merlot, Cabernet Sauvignon, Chardonnay, Riesling, Zinfandel, etc.) had collapsed into one "Wine Alcoholic Beverage"; all 8 of USDA's distilled-spirit rows (rum/vodka/whiskey at various proofs, plus the generic gin/rum/vodka/whiskey blend) had collapsed into one "Distilled Alcoholic Beverage," destroying the one distinction (rum vs. vodka vs. whiskey) that actually matters for picking the right spirit. Root cause: `split_prep_method()`'s strategy 2 (see its own docstring) only ever kept a trailing comma-clause when it matched a known cooking/raw/storage term -- when nothing matched (a varietal name, a brand, a proof level), the clause was silently discarded and the row collapsed to bare `short_name`, merging it with every other row sharing that same truncated prefix. Fixed at the actual mechanism this time rather than adding a fourth hand-verified one-off dict: when no clause matches a known term, the function now keeps the full, untruncated `name` as base_name (matching what strategy 3's own already-existing fallback already did in a different case) instead of discarding real information. Real, measurable impact confirmed by direct query: the count of (base_name, source, category) groups sharing 2+ distinct rows with no prep_method to differentiate them dropped from 295 to 92 across the ENTIRE database, not just alcohol -- meaning this bug had been silently erasing real distinguishing information well beyond what anyone had reported yet. The two earlier narrow fixes (`CHEESE_VARIETY_RESTORE`/`restore_dropped_cheese_variety()`) became dead code once the general fix landed -- their own lookup keys (the old, truncated intermediate base_name values) no longer occur anywhere in the pipeline -- so both were removed rather than left unreachable. Verified directly against the rebuilt database: all 8 beer variants (including all 3 branded ones) now have distinct base_names; all 36 wine varietals distinct; all 8 distilled-spirit rows distinct; the chicken-egg and cheese-variety fixes both re-confirmed still correct; the Food Distribution Program suffix fix still confirmed gone. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit` (clean; pure Python/data change). The remaining 92 collapsed groups haven't been individually audited -- some may be legitimate (e.g. two rows from the same source that really are the same food) -- not chased further proactively (see Next steps).

**A second, much larger Alcohol miscategorization found and fixed the same day, reported directly right after the varietal fix above: "The Beverages category still lists alcohol beverages in it... mixed drinks, and various other things, and some are even juices."** Confirmed by direct query this went well beyond USDA: 264 rows across 6 of the 7 sources (only Australia_AFCD's own alcohol content was already fully correct) carried the Bev category's own `Alcoholic` subcategory tag but had never actually been moved to the dedicated `Alcohol` top-level category the way Germany_BLS/UK_CoFID/Australia_AFCD's alcohol content already was -- USDA in particular had ZERO rows in `Alcohol` at all, meaning its ENTIRE wine/beer/spirit/cocktail contribution (every varietal and brand just fixed above, plus daiquiris, pina coladas, whiskey sours, coffee liqueurs, hard cider) had been sitting in Bev the whole time, right alongside Canada_CNF rows whose own names literally start with the word "Alcohol." Fixed with a new `reclassify_bev_alcoholic_to_alcohol()` in `scripts/build_food_reference_db.py`, reusing the EXISTING `Alcoholic` subcategory keyword match (`SUBCATEGORY_RULES["Bev"]`) rather than hand-listing all 224 rows that needed to move -- checked directly, that match is already correct for all of them. A hand-verified `BEV_ALCOHOL_FALSE_POSITIVES` set excludes the ~40 confirmed real false positives from that same keyword list (explicitly non-alcoholic juice/soda/mixer products that happen to contain "cocktail"/"beer"/"ale"/"wine," several ironically matching because "alcohol" is a substring of "alcohol-free"). **A real miss in the first pass, caught only by re-querying the rebuilt database rather than trusting the exclusion list was complete**: ~88 French "Eau minérale..." (mineral water) rows had ALSO moved, landing in "Beer & Cider," because "minérale" (French for "mineral," an adjective ending in "-ale") tripped the same "ale" keyword collision already flagged once this session in the food-name-grouping work ("Matter"/"Fat" false cognates) -- this had gone completely undocumented in the original investigation despite being visible in the very same query output, an oversight caught by systematically re-scanning the full list of newly-moved rows rather than spot-checking a sample. Fixed with a second, general guard (`if "minéral" in name.lower(): return effective_category`) rather than hand-listing 88 individual strings, which risks missing one the same way the first pass missed all of them. Verified exhaustively this time, not just spot-checked: exported and read every one of the 134 rows that ended up moved (all confirmed genuinely alcoholic -- wine, beer, spirits, liqueurs, sake, cocktails, hard cider) and every one of the 126 rows that stayed in Bev (88 mineral water + the original 38 hand-verified non-alcoholic exclusions, matching exactly). Real, unrelated composite dishes that merely CONTAIN alcohol as an ingredient (cheese fondue, stollen, pralines with alcoholic filling, zabaione) were confirmed correctly untouched -- they live in Mixed/Baked/Sweets, never Bev, so this fix's own `effective_category != "Bev"` guard never reaches them, which is the intended, narrower scope (moving alcoholic BEVERAGES, not every dish that happens to contain alcohol). Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit` (clean; pure Python/data change).

**A real gap in the fix above, reported directly right after it: "in the Beverages category, I still see a Alcoholic [subcategory]... Alcoholic shouldn't be listed in beverages at all."** Confirmed: the category-level fix decides whether a row MOVES to Alcohol, but every row it deliberately keeps in Bev (the mineral water + 38 hand-verified non-alcoholic items) still had its subcategory computed by the same keyword match that incorrectly flagged it as alcoholic in the first place -- the category fix never touched the subcategory badge those rows display, so "Alcoholic" was still showing on root beer, ginger ale, juice cocktails, and mineral water. Fixed with `bev_alcoholic_false_positive_subcategory()`, reusing the exact same set of already-hand-verified names from the category fix (mineral water -> "Water"; ginger ale/root beer -> "Soft Drinks"; juice cocktails -> "Juice"; alcohol-free wine/beer/aperitif and whiskey-sour-mix-without-whiskey -> "Other") rather than re-deriving the list. Verified directly: `Bev` category now has zero rows with subcategory `Alcoholic` (confirmed via `GROUP BY subcategory` -- the bucket is gone entirely, redistributed into Water/Soft Drinks/Juice/Other as appropriate). Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit` (clean).

**The branded-alcohol question resolved into something more useful than branded data, 2026-08-02, same day, after the person pushed back on how thin real branded coverage turned out to be ("a few Budweiser beers is all we really have?").** Confirmed via an exhaustive re-scan (every Alcohol-category row checked for an ALL-CAPS brand-name pattern, not just guessing common brand names) that this really is the full extent of it: 3 branded USDA beer rows, zero branded wine, zero branded spirits, across all 7 sources. Root cause explained rather than just reported: these are national food-composition (lab-measurement) databases built for dietary research, not retail/barcode product databases -- alcohol has historically been exempt from the FDA nutrition-labeling requirements that populate branded entries for other categories (confirmed 100+ real branded ENTRIES elsewhere in this same database -- Ocean Spray, V8, Snapple, Silk, Slimfast -- so the gap is alcohol-specific, not a database-wide limitation).

The person's own follow-up reframed the real question productively: for PLAIN distilled spirits (vodka/gin/whiskey/rum/tequila), does calorie/nutrient content actually vary by brand, or mostly by proof? Researched via WebSearch rather than assumed: confirmed real -- distillation strips out virtually all non-ethanol content, so an 80-proof spirit is ~97 kcal/1.5oz regardless of brand or base ingredient (grain, potato, agave, sugarcane all distill down to the same thing); the one real exception is FLAVORED spirits, where post-distillation sugar/flavoring genuinely does vary by brand (not chased here -- a real future gap if anyone logs one, but out of scope for this pass). The person's own separate intuition about aging ("oxidation... buildup of oxides") wasn't quite the right mechanism but was directionally right: real research confirms barrel-aged spirits (whiskey, aged rum, tequila añejo/reposado, brandy/cognac) pick up real congeners (esters/aldehydes/tannins from the wood, not oxides) that unaged spirits (vodka, gin, blanco tequila, white rum) don't, and congener load is linked in the literature to worse hangover severity -- a real, health-relevant distinction, just not one any of these 7 databases tracks as a nutrient, and not something that varies brand-to-brand within "aged" vs. "unaged" the way it might for beer.

Net conclusion, agreed with the person before building anything: chasing branded wine/spirits data was the wrong fix (the existing generic entries are already scientifically accurate for any brand at that proof); what was actually missing was the ability to correctly LOG which real category someone drank (letting the app's own pattern-finding work the way it's meant to -- see this file's core-purpose framing). Beer was confirmed as the one place brand genuinely would still matter (real recipe-driven calorie/carb variance between products), left as a real, separate, not-yet-pursued gap.

**Implemented: 5 new synthetic aged/unaged spirit entries, added directly in `scripts/build_food_reference_db.py` rather than sourced from the xlsx (confirmed via direct query these don't exist in any of the 7 real sheets)** -- Tequila Blanco/Reposado/Añejo, Rum Light-White/Dark-Aged. This is a genuinely new kind of change for this file: every previous fix this session relabeled/recategorized a real, already-existing workbook row; these are the first entries with no source-workbook row behind them at all. Handled carefully to keep that honest: each variant's full nutrient (35 codes) and D1-D6 score (24 sub-criteria) profile is a straight, unmodified duplicate of a real row's own already-computed values (tequila variants copy USDA's own generic "distilled, all (gin, rum, vodka, whiskey), 80 proof," food_id 7304 -- the best real analog for a neutral spirit regardless of base plant material; rum variants copy USDA's own real "distilled, rum, 80 proof," food_id 7306, directly) -- never independently invented, consistent with the research above that these numbers genuinely don't differ. `source` is `'Derived'`, deliberately not `'USDA'` or any real source name, so the data's actual provenance (real official numbers, applied by this app rather than measured/submitted by any of the 7 agencies) stays honest and visible in the raw data. `food_id` starts at 900001, far outside the real workbook's own range (confirmed max real food_id: 27980) -- no collision risk.

**A real bug caught before it shipped, not after**: the app's own `buildScopeClause()` (`lib/db.ts`) defaults every category with real USDA coverage to `source = 'USDA'` only -- since Alcohol has 67 real USDA rows, this would have silently hidden all 5 new `'Derived'` entries from the default browsing/search experience the moment they were added, the exact kind of "looks empty but isn't" bug already hit twice this session (Alcohol/Algae/Brewing's own zero-USDA-coverage bug; the sticky-header crash). Caught by tracing the actual query path before declaring this done, not by waiting for another bug report. Fixed with a one-line change: the USDA-only branch now filters `source IN ('USDA', 'Derived')` instead of just `'USDA'` -- confirmed via direct query that all 3 tequila variants now appear under Alcohol's own default Spirits & Liqueurs scope. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit` (clean) and direct SQL query of the rebuilt database (calorie values confirmed identical to both template rows, exactly as intended). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**A real, substantial Alcohol browsing cleanup, 2026-08-02, same day, driven by three direct follow-up requests in the same conversation.** (1) "There shouldn't be anything listed yet in the beer and cider unless they scan it in later... all beers and other canned alcoholic beverages need to be considered scan only" -- unlike plain distilled spirits (where proof alone determines nutrition, so a generic entry is already honest), beer/cider vary by real recipe, and this app has no barcode-scan feature yet to pull a specific product's real numbers; the person's own explicit call was to hide these from browsing entirely until that feature exists, rather than let someone pick a generic "beer" that doesn't represent what they actually drank. Cider explicitly included in this same "canned" reasoning. (2) "There shouldn't be a Cocktails & Mixed in Alcohol." (3) "In Spirits & Liqueurs, the names of these things are so long that I can't tell why they are different at all... Is there really no difference between vodka, whiskey, rum, and gin for the proof levels?"

Investigated (3) before touching anything, since it changes what "fixing" even means here: WebSearch confirmed distillation strips out virtually all of a spirit's base-ingredient content, so plain vodka/gin/rum/whiskey at the same proof are nutritionally near-identical -- and this database's OWN numbers independently confirm it (USDA's vodka/rum/"all" 80-proof rows and Canada_CNF's whisky/rum/vodka 40%-ABV rows are EXACT calorie matches within each source). So the verbose, near-duplicate "Alcoholic beverage, distilled, X, Y proof" entries across 5+ sources weren't measuring different things -- they were the same measurement, restated. Fixed at the root for the two USDA rows worth keeping as the real canonical entries: new `SPIRIT_CLEAN_RENAMES` in `scripts/build_food_reference_db.py` renames them to `"Vodka, 80 Proof"` / `"Whiskey, 86 Proof"`; a 6th `SYNTHETIC_SPIRIT_VARIANTS` entry, `"Gin, 80 Proof"`, fills the one real gap (no source has a standalone gin entry outside the generic bucket) by copying the same food_id 7304 template the tequila variants use, since USDA's own combined bucket already treated gin as equivalent. Rum needed no new clean entry -- the aged/unaged Derived variants added earlier the same day already serve that role.

Every now-redundant near-duplicate (the generic "all (gin,rum,vodka,whiskey)" buckets at 5 proof levels, and each source's own separate vodka/gin/rum/whisky rows measuring the same real thing) is excluded from browsing via a new `ALCOHOL_HIDDEN_BASE_NAMES` set in `lib/db.ts`, applied inside `buildScopeClause` -- NOT deleted or recategorized in the reference data itself, which stays complete (useful later if a real scan feature or higher-proof/overproof use case wants that source diversity back). The same mechanism handles (1) and (2): a new `ALCOHOL_SUBCATEGORIES_PENDING_SCAN_FEATURE` set hides "Beer & Cider" and "Cocktails & Mixed" entirely from `getReferenceSubcategories`' own Alcohol results, so neither ever appears as a pickable type.

**A real gap found while verifying (2), not assumed fixed just because the subcategory was hidden**: `SUBCATEGORY_RULES` for Alcohol checks "Spirits & Liqueurs" keywords (whisky/rum/gin/tequila/etc.) BEFORE "Cocktails & Mixed" -- so "Alcoholic beverage, whiskey sour" and "Alcoholic beverage, tequila sunrise" matched on the spirit name first and landed in Spirits & Liqueurs, never reaching the cocktail check at all, meaning they'd have stayed visible even with Cocktails & Mixed hidden. The person caught this directly and named both by name before this fix went in ("Whiskey Sour and Tequila Sunrise are names of mixed drinks. Mixed drinks shouldn't be part of this"), which matched an audit already underway -- confirmed by hand-reading every row in Spirits & Liqueurs AND Wine & Champagne (not just guessing from names) and finding a fuller set of the same leak: Canada_CNF's own "cocktail, daiquiri"/"cocktail, pina colada"/"cocktail, whisky sour mix... whisky added" rows, France_Ciqual's "Cocktail à base de whisky," Germany_BLS's "Cocktail, Gin and tonic"/"Cocktail, Tequila sunrise," and France_Ciqual's "Kir royal (au champagne)" hiding in Wine & Champagne the same way. Also caught, same reasoning extended one step further (a flavored, manufactured ready-to-drink product is the same "not scannable yet, not a plain well-defined thing" case as beer/cider even when it isn't literally beer): Canada_CNF's "Vodka cooler, fruit flavours" and "Alcohol, wine cooler," Germany_BLS's "Alcopops," and Germany_BLS's alcoholic "Egg nog" (a mixed dairy+spirit drink, not a single spirit). All added to the same `ALCOHOL_HIDDEN_BASE_NAMES` set.

Genuinely different real wine/liqueur products (fortified wines, sherry, vermouth, coffee/cream/fruit liqueurs, brandy/cognac, aquavit, arrack, mirin, sake) were deliberately left untouched after reading the full list by hand -- wine's real distinguishing factor (varietal/sweetness/style) is already captured by the existing entries and isn't the same "near-duplicate measurement" problem the plain spirits had, and liqueurs each have real, recipe-driven variance closer to beer than to plain vodka, so consolidating them the way the plain spirits were consolidated would have been guessing, not verifying. Final visible Alcohol count after every exclusion: 215 rows (down from 297 total in category, most of the delta being the hidden Beer & Cider/Cocktails & Mixed subcategories). Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean) and direct SQL simulation of the app's own query logic for both the subcategory list and the Spirits & Liqueurs/Wine & Champagne food lists. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**Bev's own Juice subcategory cleaned up the same way, 2026-08-02, once the Alcoholic-subcategory fix above was confirmed working on-device.** Reported directly: "eliminate name branded and from concentrate from view. Also the chilled, includes from concentrate, low sodium, anything with added calcium, all of the smoothie stuff from Bolthouse Farms, and Naked Juice, and odwalla, also blends of juices need to be removed from view. If it isn't just juice from a whole food fruit, it shouldn't be viewed in this list." A much bigger cleanup than Alcohol's -- 389 rows across all 7 sources, hand-reviewed row by row (not pattern-matched) against that stated rule: removed brand names (OCEAN SPRAY, V8 SPLASH, BOLTHOUSE FARMS, NAKED JUICE, ODWALLA, MOTTS, CONCORD, REAL LEMON), anything "from concentrate" or "chilled," anything fortified (added vitamin C/ascorbic acid/calcium/sugar/salt -- but explicitly NOT the "without added X"/"no salt added" negations, which are exactly the plain versions worth keeping), every blend of 2+ fruits/vegetables, every "juice drink"/"cocktail"/"nectar"/"cordial"/"spritzer"/"fruit juice beverage," Japan_MEXT's own "reconstituted"/dilution-percentage tiers (kept their own "straight fruit juice" -- their term for undiluted, unblended, 100% juice), canned-fruit-preserved-in-juice products (the fruit itself, not a juice beverage), and "Kale juice, powder" (dehydrated powder, not juice).

Implemented as an ALLOWLIST (`BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES` in `lib/db.ts`, ~133 entries), not a denylist -- deliberately the more conservative choice given how emphatic the ask was and the category's own scale; a denylist trusts every future addition to be automatically fine, an allowlist doesn't. A real complication found while building it, not assumed away: many rows share one base_name despite being nutritionally different -- all 7 of USDA's "Apple juice" rows (with/without added ascorbic acid, canned/frozen-concentrate) collapse to base_name "Apple juice," 3 of them even sharing prep_method "Canned" too, meaning neither base_name nor prep_method could isolate the one plain row from the other 6. Filtered by the full `name` column instead (checked in `buildScopeClause`, scoped to `category === 'Bev' && subcategory === 'Juice'` only -- every other Bev subcategory untouched), which is precise regardless of what base_name/prep_method a row happens to share with something that should stay hidden. Six such collided base_names found and handled this way: Apple juice, Grape juice, Orange juice, Pineapple juice, Tomato juice, White Grapefruit Juice (all USDA).

The allowlist itself was extracted directly from the source data with `sed`, by exact line number, rather than hand-retyped, specifically to avoid a transcription error across ~133 entries -- verified afterward by rebuilding the exact same list into a real SQL query and confirming it matches precisely 133 real rows (zero typos causing a silent non-match). Spot-checked the "Apple juice" collision specifically post-filter: only the genuinely plain USDA and Germany_BLS rows resolve now, not any of the other 5. A final broad re-scan for brand/concentrate/cocktail/drink/smoothie keywords among the 133 kept rows found exactly one match, "Pomegranate juice, ready-to-drink" (Canada_CNF) -- confirmed a false positive from the scan itself (its own "ready-to-drink" phrasing matching a bare "drink" substring check), not a real leak; the row is plain single-fruit juice with no other red flag, correctly kept. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**The 133 figure caused real confusion, reported directly the same day ("there aren't 133 listed in the juices") -- worth remembering for how this gets described in the future.** 133 is the underlying database ROW count across all 7 national sources that passed the filter; it is NOT what a person actually sees in the picker, since `searchReferenceFoodNames` shows one entry per DISTINCT base_name, and several sources measure the same real juice independently (e.g. both Canada_CNF and USDA have their own "Blackberry juice, canned" row, collapsing to one visible "Blackberry juice" entry). The real, user-visible count should be **103** distinct selectable juices.

**A real, separate bug is why the actual on-device count was neither number -- reported directly, "I 19 jucies listed."** 19 is a very specific number -- confirmed via direct query it's exactly the count of the allowlist's own USDA-sourced entries alone. Root cause: `resolveEffectiveUsdaOnly()` defaults every category with real USDA coverage to `source = 'USDA'` only (the same mechanism already fixed once for Alcohol/Algae/Brewing, which have ZERO USDA coverage and so already bypass it) -- Bev has plenty of real USDA rows, so this stayed on and silently crushed the whole cross-source-curated `BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES` list (deliberately spanning all 7 sources -- German lingonberry/elderberry/quince juice, Japanese yuzu/sudachi/kabosu citrus, etc.) down to just its 19 USDA rows, hiding the other 84 already-verified entries. `resolveEffectiveUsdaOnly()` widened to also take `subcategory` (all 3 call sites updated) and now returns `false` unconditionally for `category === 'Bev' && subcategory === 'Juice'` -- the usdaOnly default exists specifically to avoid near-duplicate "Spinach x7" entries across sources, but that concern is already handled here by the allowlist itself (reviewed name-by-name, cross-source duplicates like "Blackberry juice, canned" from both Canada_CNF and USDA deliberately kept since they're the same real food independently measured, not noise) -- so forcing usdaOnly for this one subcategory only threw away real, wanted variety for no benefit. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device** -- the expected post-fix count is 103; if it still doesn't match, don't assume this fix alone is sufficient, check further.

**A close, row-by-row read-through of the juice list the same day turned up one real data error and two naming near-duplicates -- all confirmed against real values, not assumed from the name alone.** The person asked pointed factual questions (what are "juice sacs," why the quotation marks in Japan_MEXT names, what does "straight fruit juice" mean, what's "white flesh type") and flagged several entries as looking redundant. Answered the factual ones directly (juice sacs = the real juice-bearing vesicle tissue inside a citrus segment, closer to "whole fruit" than a pressed juice; the quoted names are real Japanese citrus cultivar names, e.g. "Harumi"/"Kabosu"/"Yuzu," genuinely different fruits; "straight fruit juice" is Japan_MEXT's own real term for full-strength undiluted juice, distinct in their own data from "reconstituted" and "X% fruit juice beverage"; white vs. red flesh grapefruit is a real varietal difference, not noise).

Two of the "looks redundant" flags turned out to have a real, checkable answer rather than actually being redundant -- verified via nutrient data before responding, not assumed either way:
- **A genuine bug, not a naming nitpick**: `'Juice, tomato, canned'` (Canada_CNF, no qualifier) had been kept assuming "no qualifier = plain," the same assumption used throughout this whole allowlist -- checked its sodium directly and found 253mg/100g, essentially identical to the explicitly salted USDA/Germany_BLS rows (253mg/264mg) and nothing like the genuinely unsalted ones (10mg). Unlike fruit juice, canned tomato juice is salted BY DEFAULT in these sources' own convention, and only the unsalted variant gets called out by name -- the one place the "no qualifier = plain" rule this whole list was built on doesn't hold. Removed from `BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES`.
- **Checked and confirmed correct, not redundant**: "why does unsweetened need to be said for cranberry when it isn't for other juices" -- checked whether a bare, unqualified "Cranberry juice" exists anywhere in the database at all: it doesn't. Every real cranberry row is either explicitly "unsweetened" or a cocktail/blend with real added sugar (up to 42.5g/100g for the frozen-concentrate cocktail vs. 12.1g for the plain unsweetened one, which is just cranberry's own natural tartness). Commercial cranberry juice defaults to sweetened far more than apple/grape/orange does, so "unsweetened" is the only thing distinguishing the one plain product from the norm -- correctly kept as-is.

Two more turned out to be genuine near-duplicates worth fixing, not just cosmetically annoying -- both handled as base_name renames in `scripts/build_food_reference_db.py` (a new `JUICE_CLEAN_RENAMES`/`rename_juice_clean()`, same pattern as the earlier `SPIRIT_CLEAN_RENAMES`) so they actually merge into one entry rather than just being separately visible: USDA's "Purple/Yellow Passion-Fruit Juice" (hyphenated) merged into Canada_CNF's own "Purple/Yellow Passion Fruit Juice" (space) -- purely a hyphen-vs-space difference in how each source writes the same real thing, confirmed by checking neither name states any actual distinction; USDA's "Pomegranate juice, bottled" and Canada_CNF's "Pomegranate juice, ready-to-drink" merged into a plain "Pomegranate Juice" -- same reasoning, neither qualifier describes anything most other juices in this list aren't also true of. Rebuilt the database; confirmed via direct query all 3 pairs now correctly share one base_name each. Final visible count: **100** distinct juices (down from 103: -1 tomato, -2 from the two 2-for-1 merges, +1 from a `usdaOnly`-bypass-related recount nuance -- confirmed directly against the rebuilt database, not calculated by hand).

**A real, still-open design question, not yet decided**: several other entries the person flagged (Apple juice vs. Japan_MEXT's own "Apples, straight fruit juice"; Lemon/Lime juice vs. Japan_MEXT's own "Lemons/Limes, juice, fresh"; a whole cluster of mandarin-family entries -- Germany_BLS's "Mandarin juice" AND "Satsuma mandarin juice," Canada_CNF's "Tangerine (mandarin) juice," USDA's "Tangerine juice," plus Japan_MEXT's own 3 Satsuma-specific rows; a bare, undefined "Grape juice" sitting alongside Germany_BLS's own "Grape red juice"/"Grape white juice") are NOT naming bugs the way the passion-fruit/pomegranate ones were -- each source's own row is a real, independently measured product, and keeping cross-source diversity selectable was this list's own original design goal. Whether to consolidate these further (fewer total choices, at the cost of losing the ability to specifically pick, say, Japanese-sourced lemon juice) or leave them as deliberately separate is a real product decision, not yet made -- see the actual conversation for where this was left.

**The Japan_MEXT quotation marks explained, and a real grouping feature built around the answer, 2026-08-02, same day.** The quotes were never a data bug -- Japan_MEXT's own source convention wraps a real citrus cultivar's proper name in quotation marks within the food name itself (e.g. `Citrus, "Yuzu", juice, fresh`), the same way a varietal name might get quoted in English prose; "Harumi," "Kabosu," "Setoka," etc. are all genuinely different citrus cultivars, not a formatting artifact. Per the person's own suggestion ("separate them into their own group... and remove the quotations"), built a new mechanism rather than just stripping quotes in place, since 32 of the juice allowlist's 100 entries are Japan_MEXT-exclusive (never sharing a base_name with any other source -- confirmed via direct query before building this, since a name that DOES merge cross-source, like "Carrot juice"/"Tomato juice," must stay in normal alphabetical flow, not get pulled into a Japan-only bucket). `lib/foodNameGrouping.ts`'s `buildFoodNameGroups()` gained an optional `forcedGroups: Map<string, { groupLabel, displayLabel }>` parameter that partitions matching names out of the normal name-pattern grouping logic entirely and buckets them under a caller-chosen header instead -- a different mechanism from the existing candidate-key grouping on purpose, since this is grouping by *source*, not by shared name shape. `components/FoodLookup.tsx` gained `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS`/`buildJuiceForcedGroups()` (scoped to `category === 'Bev' && subcategory === 'Juice'` only). Verified via a hand-built Node.js port of the real grouping logic run against the actual 100-entry allowlist and the actual 32-name Japan set (both extracted directly from the two real source files, not retyped) -- confirmed all 32 land under one "Japanese" header, quotes stripped, non-Japanese entries unaffected. One real debugging detour worth remembering: the simulation initially showed only 29 of 32 matching, traced to the *test harness's own* `.trim()` call silently eating a genuine trailing space that's part of 3 real name strings ("Apples, straight fruit juice ", etc.) -- not a bug in the shipped code, which never trims these strings anywhere, confirmed by re-running the simulation with the harness fixed and getting a clean 32/32.

**Follow-up the same day, immediately after: "If it's under the Juice header, we shouldn't need the word juice in the name. Let's just have the name of the fruit listed."** The initial version only stripped the quote characters, leaving the rest of each Japan_MEXT name (including the word "juice"/"juice sacs"/"straight fruit juice") untouched -- redundant once the food already sits inside a Juice-subcategory list. Rather than a generic regex strip (risks mangling a name that has real content after the word "juice," and this file's own standing rule is to never guess on a small, checkable list), hand-curated a `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS` dict mapping each of the 32 real names directly to a clean fruit label ("Citrus, \"Yuzu\", juice, fresh" -> "Yuzu"; "Oranges, navel, juice sacs, raw" -> "Navel Orange"). Real distinguishing qualifiers were deliberately kept, not stripped along with "juice," since dropping them would have created same-label collisions or lost real information: Grapefruit's red-vs-white flesh type, the two differently-sourced Valencia orange rows (one grown in Japan, one imported from the U.S. and measured separately -- "Valencia Orange" vs. "Valencia Orange, Imported (U.S.)"), and Satsuma mandarin's three ripening-stage rows ("Satsuma Mandarin," "..., Early Ripening," "..., Normal Ripening"). `buildFoodNameGroups()`'s forced-group sort key changed from the raw name to the new display label too, so the group now reads in true alphabetical order by fruit name (Apple, Fukuhara Orange, Grape, Grapefruit... Yuzu) instead of by each entry's old "Citrus,"/"Grapefruit,"-prefixed raw name. Verified via the same Node.js-port methodology, this time extracting the real `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS` dict directly out of the live source file (`eval`'d, not retyped) and confirming all 32 labels are unique with no collisions, then re-running the grouping logic end to end -- clean. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on both touched files (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**A real stale-Metro-server report the same day, 2026-08-02: "we may have the cache problem again" -- the quote-stripping fix above wasn't showing up even after a force-stop and reload.** Checked directly rather than re-guessed at the code: the running Metro/Expo process (`node ./scripts/start-with-qr.js`, launched correctly via the real `npm start` path) had been up continuously since 10:46 AM -- over 9 hours, spanning this entire day's worth of edits -- while a force-stop-and-reload on the phone only fetches a fresh JS bundle FROM that same long-running server, which doesn't help if Metro's own transform cache is what's stale. Restarted the server cleanly (killed the process tree, relaunched via `npm start -- --clear` to force a full Metro cache rebuild rather than just a process restart) -- confirmed a fresh bundle build afterward. Matches this project's own standing troubleshooting note: when a fix stops landing on-device, check Metro's own process age before re-guessing the code.

**Straight fruit juice given a real, cited Hashimoto's advisory the same day, prompted directly by the person's own summary of blood-sugar/inflammation/thyroid-conversion/gut-permeability concerns, with an explicit ask to verify it before building anything from it.** Same discipline as the alcohol/coffee advisories: independently checked every claim via WebSearch/WebFetch rather than trusted the summary, and several pieces didn't hold up as stated. The "juice always spikes blood sugar faster than whole fruit" claim is real at the population level (Muraki et al. 2013, BMJ, three large prospective cohorts: daily juice intake tracks with up to 21% higher type 2 diabetes risk, whole fruit with lower risk; the Maastricht Study, Diabetes Care 2022, independently links fruit-juice/SSB fructose to more liver fat) but isn't a guaranteed acute effect -- a 2025 randomized crossover trial in adults with type 2 diabetes found no real difference in glucose/insulin response between orange juice and whole orange pieces eaten with a meal at matched sugar content; the more consistent risk factor across the literature is portion size (juice makes it far easier to drink more sugar in one sitting than the equivalent whole fruit) rather than a fundamentally different absorption curve every time. "Insulin spikes elevate cortisol" had the mechanism backwards -- it's the glucose CRASH that follows a spike, a real hypoglycemic dip, that triggers cortisol and the other counter-regulatory stress hormones, not the spike/insulin release itself; cortisol suppressing the deiodinase enzymes that convert T4 to active T3 (favoring inactive reverse T3 instead) is real, well-established endocrine physiology, but the full chain from a single juice-drinking episode to measurably altered thyroid hormone conversion hasn't been directly tested -- presented as a plausible, mechanistically-grounded chain built from separately-established facts, not a proven outcome. Gut-permeability/fructose evidence is real but still mostly animal-model, "leaky gut" remaining a debated (not settled) entity in autoimmune research, consistent with how this document already frames that term elsewhere. The levothyroxine-timing claim turned out to be the biggest overstatement in the original summary: the best available controlled data (a real crossover RCT, cited in the same systematic review already used for the coffee advisory, PMC8002057) tested GRAPEFRUIT juice specifically at a high sustained dose and found only a 9% reduction in absorption with TSH comparable to control -- the review's own authors concluded the interaction's "relevance seems to be small" and that L-T4 patients "should not be discouraged from rational fruit juice consumption." No dedicated study exists for plain orange or other juice at all. The interaction that IS well-documented is specific to CALCIUM-FORTIFIED juice (added calcium chelates T4 in the gut) -- and this app's own Juice list already excludes every calcium-fortified entry (see the juice-cleanup work above), so that particular risk mostly doesn't apply to what's actually selectable here. New `lib/juiceAdvisory.ts` (`JUICE_ADVISORY_TITLE`/`JUICE_ADVISORY_MESSAGE`/`isJuiceFood()`, checking `category === 'Bev' && subcategory === 'Juice'`), surfaced the same non-blocking, tap-to-read way as the alcohol/coffee advisories, in the three builders whose own category allowlist includes `Bev`: Beverage, Fermentation (both already had the alcohol/coffee rows, this is a third alongside them), and Smoothie (which had neither before, since it has no Alcohol/Brewing in its own allowlist -- gained its own small `juiceAdvisoryRow`/`juiceAdvisoryText` style block, copied from Beverage/Fermentation's existing `alcoholAdvisoryRow`/`alcoholAdvisoryText`, since this was the first advisory row Smoothie Builder needed). Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on every touched/new file (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**The quote-stripping fix above turned out NOT fixed after all -- reported directly the same day, right after the "stale Metro cache" fix: "Japanese juices still all have the quotes around them."** The earlier diagnosis (stale Metro process) was wrong, and this correction is worth remembering on its own: the dev server log directly confirmed the phone HAD pulled a genuinely fresh bundle after the restart (a real `Bundled ... 1822 modules` line, with live TabHub logs following it), which ruled out caching before any code was re-examined -- exactly the kind of check that should happen before re-guessing at a cache explanation a second time. The real bug: `searchReferenceFoodNames()` (`lib/db.ts`) returns `base_name`, not the full `name` column -- but `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS` (`components/FoodLookup.tsx`) had been keyed entirely by `name` (inherited from the original `JUICE_JAPAN_EXCLUSIVE_NAMES` Set, itself built during the very first version of this feature, before compaction). The two columns differ for most of these rows -- `base_name` has its prep-method clause (", raw"/", fresh") already split off and any trailing space trimmed, and one row even gets a fuller `reorder_base_name()` rewrite ("Oranges, Valencia, straight fruit juice" -> "Orange juice, Valencia (straight)") -- so every one of the 32 dict entries silently failed to match anything actually reaching the component, and all 32 fell through to the normal, unstripped candidate-key path the whole time. This also means the ORIGINAL "put them under a Japanese header" feature, from earlier the same day, never actually worked on-device either -- confirmed by re-checking: the Node.js simulation used to "verify" it that same day tested `forcedGroups` (built from `name`) against `BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES` (also `name`) -- internally consistent, but testing the wrong list, since the real `foodNameOptions` in the running app is `base_name`, a value that simulation never touched at all. A real lesson for this kind of verification going forward: a hand-built simulation is only as good as whether it's actually exercising the same data the real code path uses, not just a plausible stand-in for it. Fixed by rekeying `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS` to the real `base_name` values (confirmed via direct query that all 32 are still exclusively Japan_MEXT-sourced at this column too, one row each, before rekeying -- the display labels themselves didn't need to change). Re-verified this time against the ACTUAL query path -- extracted the real 122-entry `BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES` array straight from `lib/db.ts`, ran the real `WHERE name IN (...)` -> `SELECT DISTINCT base_name` query against the rebuilt database to reproduce the exact 100-entry list `foodNameOptions` would contain, and ran the real grouping logic against THAT -- all 32 now match, group correctly, and no quote character survives anywhere in the resulting 100-entry list (confirmed by an explicit scan of every non-Japanese entry too, not just the 32 target ones). Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean, one pre-existing unrelated warning elsewhere in the file). **Not yet exercised on-device** -- Metro's own file-watcher should pick this up via ordinary Fast Refresh (the process is fresh, restarted minutes earlier for the unrelated cache scare above), but given this exact feature has now been wrongly declared "working" once already this session, treat it as unconfirmed until actually seen on the phone.

**Confirmed on-device the quotes are gone, then a real scope correction the same day: "I was thinking that the Japanese fruit juices listed would just be those that have a Japanese name. All others outside of those that match a USDA or other English entry should be used."** Two separate real issues, both fixed together. First, the original "Japanese" group was built from Japan_MEXT EXCLUSIVITY (does any other source measure this exact base_name), not from whether the fruit itself has a Japanese name -- Apple, Grape, Grapefruit, Lemon, Lime, Navel Orange, Valencia Orange, Passion Fruit, and Pineapple only ended up Japan_MEXT-exclusive because this database's own source coverage happens to be uneven, not because they're Japanese fruits; "Seminole" (a Florida-bred tangelo named for the Seminole people) was in there for the same reason. Narrowed `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS` from 32 down to the 18 entries that are actually distinctively Japanese-named (the citrus cultivars -- Harumi, Hassaku, Hyuga-natsu, Iyo, Kabosu, Kawachi-bankan, Kiyomi, Natsudaidai, Sanbokan, Setoka, Shiikuwasha, Shiranuhi, Sudachi, Yuzu, Fukuhara -- plus Satsuma Mandarin's three rows). Second, and the direct cause of "the other juices are just labeled as Juice": once removed from the forced group, the excluded common-fruit entries fell into the EXISTING normal candidate-key grouping (`lib/foodNameGrouping.ts`) -- and 62 of the Juice subcategory's 100 real base_names literally end in the word "juice," so that word alone was forming one dominant, unhelpful mega-group covering most of the subcategory (telling a browser nothing they don't already know from the subcategory heading). Added `'juice'` to `GROUP_STOPWORDS`, checked database-wide first per this file's own standing practice for every stopword addition -- the only other places "juice" is even a last word are Mixed (3 rows), Alcohol (1), Meat (1), each a single unrelated composite dish, so no real group elsewhere is lost. Verifying the fix surfaced one more real, smaller instance of the same class of bug already documented above for "dough"/"gluten-free": "Lemon juice, canned or bottled" and "Lime juice, canned or bottled" then formed their own 2-item group keyed on "bottled," leaving each member's stripped label dangling on a conjunction ("Lemon juice, canned or"). Added `'bottled'` to `GROUP_STOPWORDS` too, same database-wide check first (every other "X, bottled" row is either already excluded from browsing by the earlier juice-cleanup pass, or the one remaining real pair -- two bottled mineral waters -- loses nothing by showing as plain ungrouped singletons instead). Net result, re-verified against the real 100-entry base_name list end to end: the Japanese group now holds exactly the 18 genuinely Japanese-named entries; the excluded common fruits (Navel/Valencia Orange, Grapefruit's red/white flesh variants, Seminole, Sour Orange) now blend naturally into small real clusters alongside their non-Japanese siblings ("Oranges: Navel, juice sacs / Valencia, imported from the U.S.A., juice sacs") instead of one giant "Juice" bucket or a misleading "Japanese" one. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean) and a corrected Node.js simulation run against the real query path (this time reading the right JSON field after an earlier version of the same verification script silently compared against `undefined` due to a `r.name`/`r.base_name` mismatch -- caught before being reported as fixed, not after). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**The Juice list itself trimmed to one row per real fruit/variety, 2026-08-02, same day -- a bigger, direct follow-up: "There should just be one of each type of fruit juice, not several variations of the same fruit juice."** Unlike the grouping-display work above, this changes which rows are actually SELECTABLE, not just how they're labeled. Checked with the person first, since it's a real judgment call with a genuine tradeoff either way: several of the "variations" are actual named varieties, not just processing differences (Grape comes in Red/White, Grapefruit in Pink/White, Orange in Navel/Valencia, Passion Fruit in Purple/Yellow) -- confirmed the decision was to keep real named varieties as their own separate entries, and only collapse true processing-only duplicates (canned vs. plain, +vitamin C vs. not, multiple sources measuring the identical product) down to one representative row each.

Rebuilt `BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES` (`lib/db.ts`) from ~101 distinct base_names down to 73: one row per plain fruit (Acerola, Apple, Apricot, ... Tomato -- 46 of them), the 6 real named-variety pairs kept separate (Red/White Grape, Pink/White Grapefruit, Purple/Yellow Passion Fruit), 4 Japan_MEXT-exclusive-but-NOT-Japanese-named entries (Navel Orange, Valencia Orange, Seminole, Sour Orange -- these are just Japan_MEXT-sourced in this database, not actually Japanese fruits, so they stay outside the Japanese group as their own plain entries), and the 18-entry Japanese-cultivar group unchanged. Two real merges along the way, not just deletions: "Sour cherry juice" (Germany_BLS) folded into "Cherry juice, tart" (USDA) -- the same species (Prunus cerasus) under its European vs. American common name, not a real distinct variety the way Sweet Cherry is; "Mandarin juice"/"Tangerine (mandarin) juice" folded into plain "Tangerine juice" -- used interchangeably by most people, no clean line between them the way Navel/Valencia orange has. "Satsuma mandarin juice" (Germany_BLS, a non-Japanese-sourced row that would have sat confusingly right next to the actual Japanese Satsuma entries) was dropped outright in favor of the already-kept Japan_MEXT "Satsuma mandarins, straight fruit juice" -- Satsuma is a genuine Japanese place name, so the same real product reads more honestly kept under the Japanese header rather than duplicated outside it.

New `scripts/build_food_reference_db.py` renames for the 4 Japan_MEXT-but-not-Japanese-named entries, extending `JUICE_CLEAN_RENAMES`/`NAME_CATEGORY_OVERRIDES`: "Citrus, Seminole, juice sacs" -> "Seminole", "Citrus, sour oranges, juice, fresh" -> "Sour Orange", "Oranges, navel, juice sacs" -> "Navel Orange", "Oranges, Valencia, straight fruit juice" -> "Valencia Orange" (previously "Orange juice, Valencia (straight)" from an earlier rename this same session -- the "(straight)" qualifier stopped meaning anything once this became the only Valencia-orange row left in the app's own allowlist, since the "imported from the U.S.A." sibling was dropped as a near-duplicate of the same variety). One real debugging detour: the Valencia rename didn't take effect on the first rebuild -- traced to `NAME_CATEGORY_OVERRIDES`, a separate full-name-keyed dict that runs AFTER `rename_juice_clean()` in the pipeline and unconditionally wins for the handful of rows it covers (this exact row already had its own override entry from an earlier session, silently clobbering the new rename). Fixed by updating the override's own target string directly rather than leaving a dead, unreachable entry in `JUICE_CLEAN_RENAMES`. Rebuilt `assets/data/foods_reference.db` twice (once before finding the override conflict, once after), re-ran `add_food_aliases.py` both times, confirmed via direct query all 4 renames land correctly.

Verified end to end against the real, rebuilt database rather than assumed: extracted the actual new 76-entry `name` list from the live source, ran the real `WHERE name IN (...)` -> `SELECT DISTINCT base_name` query to get the true 73-entry browsable list, confirmed all 18 Japanese labels still resolve (zero missing), and ran the real grouping logic against it end to end. Final shape: the Japanese group holds its 18 entries; three small, real sub-groups form naturally (Coconut: mature/young; Grape: red/white; Orange: plain/Valencia); everything else is a single clean, ungrouped entry -- no mega-groups, no near-duplicate-looking repeats. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean) on every touched file. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**A real, general UI bug found the same day, reported as "cucumber juice and elderberry juice aren't subs of Coconut... Honeydew melon juice isn't a sub of grape... from Kiwi fruit juice down to onion juice don't need to be in the Japanese group... a bunch of non orange things in the Orange group."** The underlying data/grouping logic was already correct -- none of these were ever actually assigned that `groupLabel` -- but `InlineSelectList.tsx` (the shared component behind every grouped list in the app, not just Juice) rendered an ungrouped row with the exact same plain style as a real group member, so an ungrouped item sitting right after a header purely by alphabetical coincidence ("Cucumber juice" sorting right after the "Coconut" group, "Passion fruit juice" sorting right after "Orange") visually read as if it belonged there. The hand-rolled sticky-header overlay already tracked this correctly (it clears the moment the topmost visible row has no `groupLabel`), but that's a small, easy-to-miss text change at the very top of the screen, not something that stops the row itself from looking glued to the group above it. Fixed generally, not Juice-specifically: `renderItem` now checks whether the current row is the first ungrouped item after a header or a real grouped member (comparing against `options[index - 1]`) and gives it a visibly thicker top border (`itemUngroupedStart`) -- flagged only at the actual transition point, not repeated through a run of several ungrouped rows in a row. Benefits every category that uses this grouping feature (Dairy, Meat, Veg, etc.), not just Juice, since the same ambiguity was always latent there too -- it just hadn't been reported yet, likely because those categories have enough real groups packed close together that an isolated header rarely sits for long before the next one. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**The Coconut Water young/immature split, questioned the same message ("water young/immature isn't really a thing people are specifically doing, is it?"), kept as-is rather than merged.** Young (green) coconut water is a real, specifically marketed product in its own right -- the "young Thai coconut" sold whole in grocery stores, and brands like Vita Coco built around exactly this distinction -- arguably more commonly sought after by name than "mature" coconut water is, not less. Treated the same as the other real named-variety pairs already kept separate (Red/White Grape, Pink/White Grapefruit, Navel/Valencia Orange, Purple/Yellow Passion Fruit) rather than merged down to one generic entry.

**The InlineSelectList visual fix above reported as still not showing on-device, same day, despite a force-stop-and-reload.** Re-checked the actual logic (index/previous-item comparison, `groupLabel`/`isHeader` conditions) rather than assuming a code bug -- traced through it by hand against the real 73-entry Juice list and it should correctly flag "Cucumber juice"/"Honeydew melon juice"/etc. Checked the dev server's own log before concluding anything: every `Bundled` entry since the last full `--clear` restart several hours earlier was a small "(1 module)" incremental update, never a real full-module cold bundle -- meaning the reported "force stop and reload" likely never actually re-fetched a fresh bundle from Metro, the same class of staleness already documented twice earlier this session. Restarted Metro cleanly again (killed the whole process tree, relaunched via `npm start -- --clear`). Given this exact "I reloaded and it's still stale" pattern has now recurred multiple times in one session even with the documented `npm start`-only launch discipline already being followed, it may be worth investigating a more reliable on-device refresh path (a real Expo dev-client cache-clear step, not just backgrounding/reopening the app) the next time this comes up, rather than continuing to treat each occurrence as a one-off.

**The caching diagnosis above was wrong -- confirmed directly the same day: "I fully closed the app from recent apps... force stopped and saw that it pulled down a new bundle all the way through 100%. The same problems continue to exist."** A real, genuine cold reload, and the dev server's own log independently confirms it (`Bundled 16558ms node_modules/expo-router/entry.js (1822 modules)` -- a real full bundle, not a small incremental one), so this was never a caching issue the second time. Rather than keep re-checking the same border-based logic for a bug that couldn't be found by re-reading it, replaced the fix's whole approach as the more robust move: the border-on-transition-only signal depended on comparing each row against its neighbor and was easy to miss even if rendering correctly (one row, among many identical-looking rows, with a slightly thicker top edge). Replaced with a persistent background tint (`${tabColor}1A`, ~10% opacity) applied to EVERY row that carries a real `groupLabel` -- not just the boundary row -- so a real group reads as a continuously colored block for its whole run, and reverts to the plain background the instant `groupLabel` is unset. This is also a structurally simpler, more directly-verifiable condition than the previous index/neighbor-comparison logic -- it depends only on `item.groupLabel`, a value already independently confirmed correct against the real database multiple times this session. Cleaned up the now-dead `itemUngroupedStart` style and unused `index` param from the previous attempt. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (clean). This is a pure JS/component change (no database involved), and Metro is confirmed already running fresh with this edit saved after its last start, so it should reach the phone via an ordinary reload -- no force-stop needed this time. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**STILL UNRESOLVED as of this handoff, 2026-08-02 -- reported again after the background-tint fix: "it still isn't fixed."** Two real rendering-layer fix attempts in `components/InlineSelectList.tsx` have now both failed to visibly change anything on-device, despite a confirmed genuine cold reload in between (server log showed a real 1822-module full bundle, not a cached one) -- meaning this is very likely NOT a Metro/caching issue at this point, and probably not a bug in the two specific things already tried either. **Do not re-attempt the same border or background-tint approach without new information -- both are already ruled out as ineffective, whatever the actual cause turns out to be.**

What's confirmed correct (verified multiple times via direct Node.js simulation against the real, rebuilt `assets/data/foods_reference.db`, not assumed): `buildFoodNameGroups()` (`lib/foodNameGrouping.ts`) correctly assigns `groupLabel` only to real group members, never to the alphabetically-adjacent ungrouped items the person keeps seeing misattributed (Cucumber/Elderberry juice near "Coconut," Honeydew melon juice near "Grape," Kiwi-through-Onion near "Japanese," Passion-through-end near "Orange"). `JUICE_JAPAN_DISPLAY_LABELS`/`BEV_JUICE_ALLOWED_NAMES` are confirmed correct against the live database. So the DATA layer isn't the problem.

What's been tried in the RENDERING layer and did NOT fix it, in order:
1. A thicker top border (`itemUngroupedStart`) on just the one row where a real group's membership ends and ungrouped rows resume -- computed via comparing `options[index-1]` against the current item.
2. A persistent background tint (`` `${tabColor}1A` ``) applied to every row carrying a real `groupLabel`, reverting to plain for anything without one -- structurally simpler, no neighbor-comparison needed, depends only on `item.groupLabel` directly.

Neither changed what the person sees, and a real fresh reload was confirmed between the two attempts (not just claimed -- independently verified via the dev server's own log). This strongly suggests the bug is NOT in `InlineSelectList.tsx`'s rendering logic at all, and time has now been spent twice guessing at fixes in the wrong place. **Real next steps, not yet done:**
1. **Get a screenshot or exact on-screen description from the person before writing any more code.** Every fix this far has been attempted blind (no on-device access this whole thread) -- worth explicitly asking for a screenshot now rather than continuing to guess-and-check, which is exactly what's burning the person's patience.
2. **Verify `FoodLookup.tsx` is actually still calling `InlineSelectList` with the options this investigation assumes.** Re-read the current `foodListOptions` computation and its render call directly rather than trusting this document's account of it -- it's possible something changed, or there's a second/different rendering path for this specific list that hasn't been checked.
3. **Verify there's only one Metro instance running and the phone is actually connected to it.** This session hit real Metro-staleness confusion twice already; it's worth ruling out something dumber like two Metro processes on different ports, or the phone's dev-client pointed at a stale IP/port, with a completely clean slate (kill every node process, confirm exactly one clean `npm start` instance, confirm the QR/IP the phone is actually using) before touching component code again.
4. **Consider that the visible bug might not be about `groupLabel` styling at all** -- re-examine with fresh eyes whether the person is even looking at the grouped/ungrouped distinction this document assumes, versus some other explanation (e.g., a stale in-memory `foodNameOptions` list on the JS side, a `sourceMap`/Hermes bytecode caching quirk specific to this device, or the reference database itself not having actually refreshed on-device despite the JS bundle being fresh -- `REFERENCE_DB_VERSION`'s own version-check-once-per-session mechanism, see this document's Status section, is a real candidate worth directly confirming rather than assumed fine).

**Favoriting wired up for all ten Food tab builders, plus Meal Builder's own "Save & Schedule for Later," 2026-08-08 -- closing both of the two real gaps this Status section's Next Steps item 1 had flagged since Meal Builder shipped.** Explicit request: "Let's wire up favoriting for the sub-builders, and the meal builders save and schedule feature to tie it into the scheduler under meals. If they only want to save the meal they created, or if they want it to be a favorite should both be available." Both halves of that -- saving the real record AND saving it as a favorite -- are independently available everywhere now, not an either/or choice.

New `lib/db.ts` types/functions, the shared foundation every builder edit below reuses: `BuilderFavoritePayload`/`BuilderFavoriteIngredient`/`BuilderFavoriteItemType` (one generic shape, not ten near-duplicates -- confirmed first, by direct reading, that all ten sub-builders' own `XIngredientInput` types are field-for-field identical, which is what makes one shared mechanism safe rather than a guess) plus `saveBuilderFavorite()`/`getBuilderFavorite()`, both reading/writing the same generic `favorites` table every other favorite already uses. Separately, `MealFavoriteComponent`/`MealFavoriteComponentsPayload`/`saveMealFavorite()`/`getMealFavorite()` for Meal Builder specifically -- a genuinely different shape (component references, not raw ingredients) coexisting under the same `item_type: 'meal'` as the OLD, pre-rebuild `MealFavoritePayload` (flat `MealIngredientInput[]`, still read by Schedule's own `matchingFavorites`/`favoriteRotatingIngredients`) -- `getMealFavorite` returns `null` for an old-shape row rather than attempting any migration (real, not-yet-shipped software with no installed users to migrate), and `app/(tabs)/schedule.tsx`'s own `favoriteBaseIngredients` was made defensive (`?? []`) so a new-shape row reaching that old code path can't crash it.

**All ten sub-builders (Side, Salad, Smoothie, Fermentation, Beverage, Snack, Baked Goods, Soup, Sauces, Handhelds) got the identical mechanical pattern**, each verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` individually as it was completed: a new `fromFavoriteId?: string` prop (mirroring each builder's own existing `editXId` prop exactly, but never marking anything as an edit -- finishing always creates a genuinely NEW saved record from the favorite's own snapshot, matching how a favorite has always worked as a reusable template elsewhere in this app); a prefill `useEffect` sourced from `getBuilderFavorite` instead of `getX`/`getXIngredients`, reconstructing each ingredient's real `ResolvedFoodSelection`/scores via `getFoodIdentity`/`getFoodScores` the same way the edit-mode effect already does; an `alsoSaveAsFavorite` checkbox (`renderFavoriteToggle()`, defaulting to checked when reached via `fromFavoriteId` itself, on the theory that reusing a favorite to build another one is a likely enough intent to default toward) inserted in the two real places a builder can finish from (the pending-ingredient card's own button row, create-mode only, and the "ready" screen) -- two locations, not one, since `saveIngredient('finish')` bypasses the ready screen entirely when triggered from the first. On a successful real save, `alsoSaveAsFavorite` independently and additionally calls `saveBuilderFavorite(itemType, payload)` in its own try/catch, so a favorite-save failure surfaces its own honest message ("X saved, favorite failed...") without ever losing or rolling back the real save that already succeeded.

**Meal Builder gained a new `favoriteId` prop** (same mechanism, sourced from `getMealFavorite` and resolving each `MealFavoriteComponent` via the already-existing `getMealComponentDisplayInfo` -- the same resolution `templateMealId`'s own effect already used, just from a favorite instead of a real logged meal), its own `alsoSaveAsFavorite` checkbox next to "Log This Now" (governs that button only), and a genuinely new second action, **"Save & Schedule for Later"** -- the Phase 3 shortcut this file's own top comment in `MealBuilder.tsx` had explicitly flagged as deferred when Meal Builder first shipped. Opens a small Hour/Minute/AM-PM time step (`schedulingTime`), reusing `lib/timeOfDay.ts`'s existing `buildTime24`/`formatTime12` helpers and the same PopoverSelect-pair-plus-AM/PM-pills shape Profile's own time pickers already use -- deliberately **today-only**, not a real date picker, since Schedule's own Meals lens (`app/(tabs)/schedule.tsx`) only supports "today, at a specific time" itself right now; giving Meal Builder more scheduling reach than Schedule's own UI has wasn't this feature's job. On confirm, **always** saves a real `saveMealFavorite()` first regardless of the `alsoSaveAsFavorite` checkbox's own state -- a deliberate, structurally-required exception to the "both independently available" design, stated plainly in the code's own comment: `scheduleMeal`'s `sourceFavoriteId` needs a real favorite/template to resume from later, so there's no way to schedule at all without one. Then calls the existing `scheduleMeal({ title, mealType, scheduledFor: <today>T<time>, sourceFavoriteId })` -- the same function Schedule's own "Pick a template or favorite" flow already calls, so a meal scheduled this way shows up on Schedule's own Meals lens identically to one scheduled there directly.

**The "reuse a favorite" read/routing side wired end-to-end**, not just the write side: `app/food-items.tsx`'s tap handler, previously showing a "coming soon" message for every favorite regardless of itemType, now routes `status === 'favorite'` taps for all ten sub-builder types into `/food` with a new `fromXFavoriteId` param each (`fromSideFavoriteId`, `fromSaladFavoriteId`, ... `fromHandheldFavoriteId`), and `itemType === 'meal'` into `/food` with `mealFavoriteId` -- written inline per itemType (not via a helper), matching the Edit button's own existing block right below it, for the same typed-routes reason that block already explains. `app/(tabs)/food.tsx` reads all eleven new params, passes each through as the matching builder's `fromFavoriteId`/`favoriteId` prop, and its own `useFocusEffect` reveal logic now jumps straight into the right builder for a favorite tap the same way it already does for an edit link or a scheduled "Log now." Delete was extended to favorites too (`app/food-items.tsx`'s Delete button, previously `status === 'saved'`-only, now also fires for `status === 'favorite'`, routing to the already-fully-generic `deleteFavorite(id)` rather than the per-builder `deleteItem` switch) -- Edit was deliberately NOT extended to favorites, since "Use this Favorite" (the tap itself) already opens a real, editable builder before anything is saved, so a separate "edit the favorite in place" concept isn't needed. A new "Favorite Meals" tile was added to the Food tab's own My Foods hub (`mealFavoriteCount`, fetched via `listFavorites(50, 'meal')` alongside the existing ten sub-builder counts) -- deliberately no "Saved Meals" tile alongside it, since Meal Builder logs a real `meals` row directly rather than saving its own standalone record the way every sub-builder does, so there's nothing to browse there except favorites.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` across every touched/new file (`lib/db.ts`, all ten builder components, `components/MealBuilder.tsx`, `app/food-items.tsx`, `app/(tabs)/food.tsx`) -- all clean, zero errors, zero new warnings. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**The Assessment ("Check-In") screen's native Stack header wasn't themed to match the app, fixed the same day.** Reported directly: "The System check in header needs to be the same background color as the app is supposed to be." Every sibling top-level Stack screen (`profile`, `purple-digest`, `food-items`, `food-item-detail`) already had `headerStyle`/`headerTintColor` set to `colors.background`/`colors.textPrimary` in `app/_layout.tsx` -- `assessment` was the one screen that had been left out, defaulting to a plain white bar with black text against everything else's dark navy. Fixed with the identical two properties, matching the existing pattern exactly. `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` clean.

**Meal Builder's Continue button now blocks entirely when there's nothing anywhere to build a meal from, 2026-08-08, same day.** Explicit request: "A meal can't be built unless there are already sides, or any combination of the other builders already created and saved. The Meal builder should not allow the Continue button to turn green and activate, and the Continue button should actually say something that causes the user to know they have to make sides or other things before a meal can be built." New `hasAnySavedComponents` state (`boolean | null`, null while the real check is still in flight) checks all eleven component categories via `Promise.all(CATEGORY_META.map(...listMealComponentOptions...))` once per mount -- sufficient without a separate focus listener, since switching to a different builder and back is a real unmount/remount of this whole component (`app/(tabs)/food.tsx`'s own lens ternary), so building a first Side elsewhere and returning always sees a fresh count. `identityReady` now requires `hasAnySavedComponents === true`, not just a chosen meal type; `handleContinuePress` gained the same check server-side (matching how every other real gate in this file already isn't purely cosmetic); the button's own label swaps from "Continue" to "Build a Side or Other Item First" once confirmed empty, and a real explanatory card (not just the button text) appears above the identity form the same way.

**A real, separate gap found and fixed while building this check, not something the person reported directly**: Handhelds Builder (the 11th sub-builder, added 2026-08-04) was already fully wired into `MealComponentType`/`listMealComponentOptions`/`getComponentDetail` (see that date's own entry), but `MealBuilder.tsx`'s own `CATEGORY_META` -- the "Add from..." grid itself -- was never updated to actually offer it as a category. Left unnoticed, the new gate would have silently ignored anyone whose only saved item was a Handheld, blocking Continue incorrectly. Added `{ type: 'handheld', label: 'Handheld', icon: 'layers-outline' }`, matching that builder's own icon in `FOOD_LENSES`. Meal Builder can now genuinely assemble from all eleven sub-builders' saved output, not ten.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` (one real fix needed: a raw apostrophe in JSX text tripped `react/no-unescaped-entities`, fixed by wrapping the string in a JS expression rather than leaving it as literal JSX children -- both clean afterward). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**LensHub now auto-opens whenever a tab is picked from TabHub (or Home's own Purple Digest shortcut), and the corner "Tap the icon button" resting prompt is gone, 2026-08-08, same day.** Explicit request: "When the Digest menu is selected from the Home screen, have it transition to the Digest page and cause the Digest LensHub menu to be automatically displayed and ready for someone to select a lens. In fact, whenever a different tab is selected from the TabHub menu, cause the same thing to happen... [then] we won't need to have the little box in the lower right (or left) corner telling them to Tap the (icon) button to select a function. The boxes that show up to tell which lens they are in should all still appear for each lens for all tabs."
