**Four new Purple Digest categories added, 2026-08-07, direct response to "this area MUST include everything at all worth knowing" plus three specific gaps named directly: healing-stages content wasn't actually in the Digest despite being fully researched elsewhere; nothing covered how Hashimoto's damages internal organs (the liver specifically named) or how fixing them helps back; and a request for "a history and milestones of Hashimoto's" plus "element interactions that create synergistic or antagonistic effects, and how food can heal a lot of those things."** `DIGEST_CATEGORY_KEYS` (`lib/digest/types.ts`) grew from 8 to 12 (`problemFoods` stays its own separate type, unchanged) -- Healing Stages, Organs & Body Systems, History & Milestones, Nutrient Interactions -- each a real new file under `lib/digest/`, wired into `index.ts`'s aggregation and `DIGEST_CATEGORY_META` the same way every existing category already is. Explicitly **not** claimed as now-exhaustive -- "everything worth knowing" isn't a state a growing medical literature ever actually reaches, and this file's own established practice (see the 2026-08-06 entry above) is to never frame a count as a ceiling. This is a real, substantial expansion (49 new entries, ~50 new independently-verified citations), not a token gesture toward the request.

**LensHub gained real scroll capability first, since 12 categories at 3 columns needs more rows than `CARD_HEIGHT` (sized to Food's own 9-option worst case) can show at once** -- explicitly offered ("if we need to make the... LensHub menu be able to scroll... let's set that up") and taken. `components/LensHub.tsx`'s own options grid is now wrapped in a plain `ScrollView` (`gridScroll: { flex: 1 }`, `contentContainerStyle={styles.grid}`) instead of a bare `View` -- the card's own outer height stays exactly what it always was; only the grid's own content can now scroll within that fixed window. Deliberately unconditional (not gated behind a per-page flag): a `ScrollView` whose content is shorter than its container behaves identically to a plain `View`, so every other page's own grid (which already fit) is unaffected. `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` clean.

**Healing Stages (13 entries, `lib/digest/healingStages.ts`) is a direct port, not new research** -- CLAUDE.md's own healing-journey-stages section already pointed at a real, fully-researched standalone Artifact ("The Healing Stages: A Grounded, Staged Food Guide," 2026-08-05/06) that had never actually been surfaced inside the app itself. Fetched the real Artifact content directly (`WebFetch` against its own `claude.ai/code/artifact/...` URL) and restructured its real stage-map, Stage 1/2/3 food lists, milestones, and three named tensions ("gut-healing foods vs. histamine load," nightshades genuinely unresolved, "detox" isn't a real physiological phase) into real `DigestEntry` records, carrying over every one of that document's own already-verified citations (AIP protocol structure, low-FODMAP response timelines, food-symptom-diary accuracy, etc.) rather than re-deriving them from scratch.

**Organs & Body Systems (12 entries, `lib/digest/organSystems.ts`) gives the liver real, sustained coverage (4 of the 12 entries) as directly requested, then covers every other organ system with real, documented Hashimoto's/hypothyroidism involvement**: the liver's own central role converting T4 to active T3 (its DIO1 enzyme is the single largest extrathyroidal contributor), the real mechanism behind hypothyroidism-driven liver enzyme elevation (membrane permeability + reduced hepatic blood flow, confirmed reversible with treatment), the real NAFLD/MASLD link (a 2025 UK Biobank cohort: 1.68x higher odds), and the real, encouraging reverse direction (improving liver health measurably helps thyroid-relevant markers back, and vice versa) -- plus a real autoimmune liver-disease overlap (PBC/AIH), cardiovascular, brain/cognitive ("brain fog," honestly flagged as a real symptom without a fully mapped mechanism yet), kidney (real, dose-graded, reversible GFR reduction), reproductive/fertility (including the real, humbling finding that levothyroxine alone doesn't fully correct antibody-driven miscarriage risk), a real autoimmune adrenal-attack entry distinct from the existing HPA-axis/cortisol one (Autoimmune Polyglandular Syndrome Type 2 / Schmidt syndrome), musculoskeletal (real, sometimes severe, always-reversible hypothyroid myopathy), and skin/hair (a real, direct hair-follicle mitochondrial mechanism).

**History & Milestones (8 entries, `lib/digest/history.ts`) is a genuinely different shape from every other category** -- real, dated turning points rather than food guidance: Hashimoto's own 1912 discovery from four thyroid specimens: the 1924 Michigan iodized-salt program (a real voluntary public-health intervention that cut goiter rates up to 90%); 1956's discovery that this was an autoimmune disease at all (a real turning point for all of immunology, not just this one disease); the 1960s-80s evolution of real thyroid lab testing (TSH radioimmunoassay -> monoclonal-antibody sandwich assays); 1985's identification of TPO as the actual antigen behind the "microsomal antibody" this app's own tracking is built around; the real, documented shift from desiccated animal thyroid to synthetic levothyroxine (driven by a real, serious potency-consistency problem, not just marketing); and the real, still-limited genetic-era understanding (first Hashimoto's-specific GWAS: 2018).

**Nutrient Interactions (12 entries, `lib/digest/nutrientInteractions.ts`) directly answers "element interactions... synergistic or antagonistic... how food can heal a lot of those things"** -- every entry ends on a real, practical food-level lever, not just a lab finding: calcium/iron (a real antagonism, genuinely more dose-dependent than old advice implied -- a 2011 trial found no effect below 800mg), vitamin C/iron (real, if diet-diluted, synergy), zinc/copper (a real supplement-level antagonism, not really a food-level one), vitamin D/K2/magnesium (a real interdependent trio, not three separate supplements), selenium/iodine (genuinely both a synergy AND a real antagonism depending on sequencing -- selenium before correcting iodine deficiency can make it worse), iron/zinc/manganese (real mineral-transport competition), vitamin A/zinc (real mechanism, honestly mixed human evidence), tea/coffee/cocoa vs. iron (a real, large, fully timing-fixable antagonism), phytates (a real blocker with real traditional fixes -- soaking/sprouting/fermentation cut the phytate:zinc ratio 81% and phytate:iron ratio 85% in one real study, a direct link back to this app's own Fermented Foods research), turmeric/black pepper (curcumin's real bioavailability increasing 2000% with piperine -- Shoba et al. 1998), fat-soluble vitamins needing real dietary fat present to absorb at all, and magnesium/B6 (a real, bidirectional synergy, confirmed in an RCT).

**Two real citation/claim mismatches caught and fixed before shipping, not after** -- the same "check your own work" discipline as the 2026-08-06 citation-linking pass, applied proactively this time rather than only in response to a direct request: the liver-damage entry's first citation candidate (NCBI's own LiverTox "Thyroid Hormone" page) turned out to be about levothyroxine-AS-A-DRUG causing rare hepatotoxicity, not hypothyroidism's own direct effect on liver enzymes -- a real, different topic than the entry's own claim, caught by actually reading what the fetched page said rather than trusting the search snippet alone; replaced with the actual right paper (Bayraktar & Van Thiel, Hepatogastroenterology). The kidney entry's Nature.com URL redirected to a login/auth-check page for automated traffic despite Scientific Reports being genuinely fully open-access -- swapped for the identical article's PMC mirror instead, which loads cleanly. A stray, unintended non-Latin character that leaked into one entry's own prose (a keyboard/encoding artifact, not a factual error) was also caught and fixed on inspection before this was called done.

`npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` both clean across all four new files, `LensHub.tsx`, `index.ts`, `types.ts`, and the whole project (only pre-existing, unrelated `app-example/` template noise remains). `PURPLE_DIGEST_VERSION` bumped. Purple Digest's own help-sheet text ("Nine categories, one evidence standard") updated to stop stating a number at all, consistent with this file's own established reasoning for why. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- the LensHub scroll behavior specifically is worth a real on-device check before trusting it fully, since it's genuinely new interaction surface (scrolling inside a popup Modal) that nothing else in this app currently does at this exact layer.

**A new standalone research Artifact, "What Happened to Food: A Correlational History of Industry, Soil & Autoimmune Disease," 2026-08-07 -- explicit request to trace "a human history of eating until now," correlate it against autoimmune/digestive disease trends, and fold in soil-nutrient decline and pesticide carcinogenicity, with real analysis and an explicit invitation for the assistant's own opinion.** Not yet folded into the app itself (like the Barcode Feasibility and original Literature Scan documents before it, this is meant for reading and discussion first) -- a real, dated timeline running from pre-1870s whole-food baseline through industrial milling (1870s)/hydrogenated fat (1911)/synthetic pesticides (1945-)/HFCS (1970s-80s) to today's ultra-processed-majority diet, with each era's real food-industry change placed directly alongside the real disease-incidence data from roughly the same window (autoimmune disease overall, IBD, celiac, T1D, Hashimoto's itself).

Covered honestly, not just favorably: the soil/nutrient-decline section presents the two landmark historical comparisons (Davis 2004, Mayer 1997) alongside a real, direct methodological critique of both (archived-soil-sample comparisons did NOT find declining soil mineral content under intensive cultivation, challenging "depleted soil" as the primary mechanism) -- concluding the better-supported explanation is the genetic "dilution effect" (breeding for yield spreads the same real nutrients across more bulk), while separately confirming real, controlled trace-mineral (zinc/copper) soil depletion under 75 years of continuous tillage in an apples-to-apples study. The pesticide section treats glyphosate as the genuinely contested case it is -- IARC's 2015 "probably carcinogenic" classification stated alongside the EU/WHO-FAO regulatory bodies that disagree, a real 2025 animal study (Ramazzini Institute) reopening the question at currently-"safe" doses, and the gut-microbiome-disruption mechanism explicitly tiered "weak" (most gut bacteria lack the pathway glyphosate targets) rather than overstated.

A real "Mechanism Bridge" section ties both threads back to this app's own already-verified Gut & Microbiome research (emulsifier-driven mucus thinning, gliadin/zonulin permeability) and the separate "disappearing microbiota" hypothesis (Blaser & Falkow) running on the same historical timeline. Closes with an explicit, clearly-labeled **opinion section** (not a citation) giving a real, considered synthesis -- naming competing explanations for rising autoimmune incidence honestly (better diagnosis, an aging population, non-food environmental exposures) rather than letting the food-industry story stand as the sole explanation, while still landing on "a real, substantial contributor operating alongside several other real contributors," explicitly invited as something to argue with rather than accept.

**A real, proactive self-check caught and fixed 3 broken/missing citation links before this was called done** -- a mistyped IARC URL (404, caught by directly fetching it rather than trusting the search-result URL verbatim) and 6 citation blocks that were drafted with real source text but no actual `href` yet (caught by a direct grep-based parity check: finding-block count vs. href count) -- all fixed with independently WebFetch-verified real URLs before republishing, not left for a future report. Favicon 🌾. **Not yet exercised by hand in the browser.**

**A new section added to the same Artifact the same day, "Whole Foods Wrongly Blamed, While Their Fabricated Replacements Got a Pass"** -- direct follow-up request to tie in real cases of a whole food taking public blame while an industrial substitute got marketed as the healthier choice, salt and margarine named directly as examples. Covered four real, documented cases: **salt** (the 1988 INTERSALT study is real and foundational, but real, more recent meta-analyses find a genuinely contested J/U-shaped mortality curve rather than a clean "less is always better" line, "salt sensitivity" is a real, well-documented ~46%-of-people individual-variation phenomenon rather than a universal effect, and -- the most directly relevant fact -- roughly 70% of modern dietary sodium comes from processed/restaurant food, only 5-6% from home salting); **butter → margarine** (the real 1948 AHA/Procter & Gamble funding history, the real, documented selective-country-reporting critique of Ancel Keys' 1958 Seven Countries Study, and margarine's own real trans-fat harm, not established until decades later); **sugar's real, documented cover story** (Kearns et al. 2016, JAMA Internal Medicine -- real internal Sugar Research Foundation documents showing the industry directly funded 1960s-70s research, including an influential 1967 Harvard review, specifically to shift blame from sugar onto fat); and **eggs/dietary cholesterol** (a real 2020 AHA science advisory explaining why the old 300mg/day limit was dropped, since dietary cholesterol\'s real effect on blood cholesterol is far smaller than assumed for most people).

**A direct, important correction made before this was published, not after**: the user\'s own framing included the popular claim that margarine is "one molecule away from plastic." Checked directly via a real science fact-check organization rather than repeated uncritically -- the claim doesn\'t hold up chemically (margarine\'s triglycerides and a true polymer like polyethylene are structurally unrelated, nothing like "one molecule" apart). Named this directly and dropped the claim from the document rather than launder it through -- the real, verified story underneath (industry funding, a selectively-reported foundational study, decades of recommending a trans-fat-laden product over a real whole food) is damning enough on its own without leaning on a claim that doesn\'t survive scrutiny.

Same self-check discipline applied again on this addition specifically: after drafting, ran a line-by-line scan (every `class="finding"` block checked for a paired `src-link` before the next `tier-badge`) and found one citation still missing its link -- fixed with the same FDA trans-fat source already used elsewhere in this same document, rather than left incomplete. `npx tsc`/`eslint` not applicable (pure HTML/CSS artifact, no app code touched). **Not yet exercised by hand in the browser.**

**The "What Happened to Food" standalone Artifact folded into Purple Digest as its own real category, 2026-08-07, same day, explicit request: "Fold the food-history artifact into Purple Digest as its own category, but leave no stone unturned for information."** New `lib/digest/foodIndustryHistory.ts` (`FOOD_INDUSTRY_HISTORY_ENTRIES`, 19 entries), wired into `types.ts` (`DIGEST_CATEGORY_KEYS` gained `'foodIndustryHistory'`) and `index.ts` (`DIGEST_CATEGORY_META` gained a "Food Industry & History" entry, icon `trending-up-outline`). Purple Digest now has 13 real categories total.

Every citation the Artifact's own Soil, Pesticides, Scapegoats, and Mechanism sections already carried was ported over unchanged (already independently verified when the Artifact was built) -- no re-research needed there. The Artifact's own Timeline section was different: its era-by-era prose had no per-fact inline citations, so rather than port unverified specific statistics, every timeline claim was independently re-verified via `WebFetch` before being included (this session's own `WebSearch` budget was already exhausted -- confirmed by a direct tool-system message on the first attempted search -- so every new fact this pass was found via `WebFetch` against real PubMed/EPA/Wikipedia pages, the same fallback method already established earlier in this document).

**Two real corrections came out of that re-verification, not just confirmation of what the Artifact already said.** The original Artifact's "worldwide autoimmune disease incidence and prevalence rising at 19.1%/12.5% per year" figure could not be traced to any real source after a genuine search effort -- dropped and replaced with a real, much more specific (and more honestly complicated) finding from Conrad et al. 2023, *The Lancet* (a 22-million-person UK cohort, PMID 37156255): some autoimmune diseases rose sharply between 2000-02 and 2017-19 (coeliac +119%, Sjogren's +109%, Graves' +107%), while Hashimoto's thyroiditis itself was measured DECREASING 19% over the same window in that same cohort -- a genuinely more nuanced picture than "autoimmune disease is rising across the board," included honestly rather than smoothed over, with the real caveat that diagnostic-coding/clinical-practice changes are a plausible alternative explanation for a measured decrease, not necessarily fewer real cases. Separately, the Artifact's own "margarine consumption peaked at 11.9 lbs/person in 1976" figure also couldn't be re-traced to a real source -- replaced with a verified Wikipedia figure (1930: ~18 lbs butter vs. ~2 lbs margarine per person; end of 20th century: ~5 lbs butter vs. ~8 lbs margarine), a real, sourced trend showing the same substitution even without the specific unverifiable peak-year statistic.

New real citations found and verified this pass, not carried over from the Artifact: Kaplan & Windsor 2021, *Nature Reviews Gastroenterology & Hepatology* (PMID 33033392, the "four epidemiological stages" of IBD -- Emergence/Acceleration/Compounding Prevalence/Equilibrium); Dinse et al. 2020, *Arthritis & Rheumatology* (PMID 32266792, ANA prevalence rise 1988-2012); Taavela et al. 2024, *Alimentary Pharmacology & Therapeutics* (PMID 37946663, Finnish coeliac disease prevalence doubling 1980-2000); Bray, Nielsen & Popkin 2004, *AJCN* (PMID 15051594, HFCS consumption rising >1000% 1970-1990, temporally linked to the obesity epidemic); Juul et al. 2022, *AJCN* (PMID 34647997, the real source of the ultra-processed-food-share-of-calories figures, confirmed via direct fetch: 53.5% in 2001-02 to 57.0% in 2017-18, minimally-processed whole food correspondingly falling from 32.7% to 27.4%); EPA's own DDT history page (1945 agricultural adoption, 1962 *Silent Spring*, 1972 US ban).

The closing "Where I Actually Land on All This" opinion section -- the piece the user directly quoted back and agreed with -- is included as its own entry (`foodhistory-opinion-synthesis`), deliberately with an empty `citations` array and a `stageNote` explicitly labeling it a stated opinion rather than a citation-backed claim, exactly matching the Artifact's own framing. `relatedIds` cross-links reach into three already-existing categories (`additive-trans-fats`/`additive-emulsifiers-cmc-polysorbate80` in Food Additives, `gut-zonulin-gliadin` in Gut & Microbiome, `nutrient-selenium`/`nutrient-iodine` in Nutrients, `problem-commercial-premade` in Problem Foods & Swaps) -- confirmed each target ID actually exists via direct `Grep` before shipping, since a dangling `relatedIds` entry fails silently (filtered out by `findDigestEntryById` returning `undefined`) rather than erroring, so a typo here would have been invisible without checking.

Per the standing decision recorded earlier this same day in this document's "Relationship to the companion book project" section: this content (like all Purple Digest / research-Artifact work) doubles as source material for the companion book, not just app content.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` across the whole project -- clean (only the pre-existing, unrelated `app-example/` template noise remains). **Not yet exercised on-device** -- worth a real on-device check of the new category specifically, given it's the largest single category addition (19 entries) since the original nine-category build.

**Two real `LensHub.tsx` fixes, 2026-08-07, same day, reported directly: "We need to make the names of the digest lenses be on two rows so they can be read, or we need shorter names for them" and "since the digest LensHub menu scrolls, it should have some characteristic that tells people to scroll it."**

1. **Longer category names now wrap to 2 lines instead of truncating.** `LensHub.tsx` gained an `itemLabelLines?: number` prop (default 1, so every other page's already-tuned single-line labels are unaffected) driving the grid item label's own `numberOfLines`. Purple Digest passes `itemLabelLines={2}`. Also switched Purple Digest's own `columns` from `3` to `2` in the same pass -- real category names ("Mitochondria & Metabolism", "Other Autoimmune Diseases") were still too long to comfortably fit even 2 lines at 3 columns' ~95px-wide column; 2 columns' ~142px width fits every real name at 2 lines without shortening any of them. This also means Purple Digest's own Info tile reverted to its usual floating bottom-right corner (`showInfoInGrid` only applies when `columns !== 2`) instead of sitting inside the grid -- the same behavior Insights/Schedule/Bio-Compass's own 2-column LensHub pages already use, not a regression.
2. **A real scroll-hint affordance, not just enabled scrolling.** `LensHub.tsx`'s grid `ScrollView` is now wrapped in a `gridWrapper` View that also renders a conditional fade-plus-chevron overlay (`canScrollMore`, tracked via `onLayout`/`onContentSizeChange`/`onScroll` against a plain ref rather than state, so a scroll drag doesn't force a re-render on every frame -- only the derived boolean flipping does) pinned to the grid's own bottom edge: a `LinearGradient` (`expo-linear-gradient`, already used elsewhere in the app -- e.g. `TabHub.tsx`) fading from transparent into `colors.menuSurface`, plus a small `chevron-down` Ionicon in the page's own `tabColor`. Shows only while real content remains below the visible window, and hides itself once scrolled to the bottom (or never appears at all on a page whose grid already fits, e.g. every page besides Purple Digest) -- `pointerEvents="none"` throughout, so it's a pure visual cue that never intercepts a tap meant for the grid item underneath it. Reset (`scrollMetrics.current` zeroed, `canScrollMore` set false) every time the popup is freshly opened, so a stale hint from a previous open (already scrolled to the bottom last time) can't flash wrong for one frame before the new `ScrollView`'s own measurements land.

Both changes live entirely in the shared `LensHub.tsx`, so any future page whose own option count grows past what fits (or whose labels run long) gets both fixes for free, not just Purple Digest. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on both touched files (`components/LensHub.tsx`, `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx`) -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**Two more `LensHub.tsx` fixes, same day, same conversation, immediate follow-up: "Make sure the names of the icons have a forced carriage return at a logical spot and that they are centered under the icons rather than being aligned left," then mid-turn: "The Info icon gets in the way being on top of the rest. Keep it as an icon of the Digest LensHub menu."**

1. **Deliberate line breaks instead of automatic wrapping.** `LensOption` (`LensHub.tsx`) gained an optional `gridLabel?: string` field -- when set, the grid tile renders this instead of `label` (which stays what's used everywhere else the option's name appears: the Info sheet's own heading, `activeLensLabel`, etc.). Purple Digest's own `LENSES` now looks up a per-category `DIGEST_GRID_LABEL_BREAKS` map, inserting a real `"\n"` (React Native renders a literal newline in Text as an actual line break) at a chosen, logical spot for every category name long enough to need one -- generally right after an "&", so both halves still read as coherent phrases ("Mitochondria &" / "Metabolism", "Other Autoimmune" / "Diseases") rather than wherever plain word-wrap happened to land. Short names that already fit on one line (Food Additives, Gut & Microbiome, Fermented Foods, Healing Stages) were deliberately left out of the map -- forcing an unnecessary break there would just leave a sparse-looking second line.
2. **Wrapped labels now center, not left-align.** `itemLabel`'s style gained `textAlign: 'center'` -- `alignItems: 'center'` on the surrounding `item` only ever centered the label's text block as a *whole* within its column; each individual line inside a wrapped block still defaulted to left-aligned. Harmless for every single-line label elsewhere in the app (a one-line block that already sizes to its own content looks identical either way).
3. **Info tile moved back into the grid at 2 columns, not just at odd column counts.** The earlier fix (switching Purple Digest to `columns={2}` for wider, more readable columns) had the side effect of reverting Info to its old floating bottom-right corner (`showInfoInGrid`'s old hardcoded rule was `columns !== 2`) -- reported directly as "gets in the way being on top of the rest" once Purple Digest's own grid grew tall enough to scroll underneath it (the floating-corner trick was only ever designed around a short, non-scrolling 2-column grid always leaving that corner blank -- not true anymore once real content scrolls there). Decoupled the two concerns: `columns` and whether Info renders in-grid are now independently settable via a new `infoInGrid?: boolean` prop (default `columns !== 2`, so every existing caller's behavior is unchanged). Purple Digest now passes `infoInGrid={true}` explicitly alongside `columns={2}` -- a combination the old hardcoded rule couldn't express. The centering math for placing Info (`blanksBeforeInfo`) only applied to odd column counts to begin with (Food's 3, centered on its own 4th row) -- extended so an even column count (Purple Digest's 2, no true "middle" to center on) skips the padding entirely and just renders Info as the next tile right after the last real category, filling whichever slot that naturally is.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on both touched files -- clean; confirmed the other six `columns={3}` LensHub call sites (Insights, Schedule, Food, Trends, Reports, Log) are unaffected by grepping every real usage before calling this done, since `infoInGrid`'s default expression depends on `columns`. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**`LensHub.tsx`'s Info tile no longer silently does nothing before a function is picked, 2026-08-07, same day, explicit request: "When I touch any Info button in any lens prior to touching a lens first, there needs to be a short message explaining they need to tap one of the functions to see the information about that function."** Previously the Info tile (both the in-grid and floating-corner variants) was simply `disabled` until `selectedOption` existed -- a real, silent no-op tap, with only a screen-reader-only `accessibilityLabel` ("Select a function to see information about it") ever actually saying so, never anything a sighted person tapping it would see. This is a shared-component fix, not Purple-Digest-specific -- it applies to every page using `LensHub` (Insights, Schedule, Food, Trends, Reports, Log, Bio-Compass, Purple Digest), matching the "any lens" scope of the request.

New `NO_SELECTION_HELP` constant (one `HelpSection`: "Choose a function first" / "Tap one of the functions above to see information about it."), shown via the same `HelpSheet` a real option's own content already opens in. The Info tile's own `disabled` condition narrowed to `Boolean(selectedOption) && !selectedOption?.help` -- disabled only for the real, different case of an option that IS selected but genuinely has no `help` authored yet for it (several pages are still rolling that out per-option, see `LensOption`'s own comment), never just because nothing's selected yet, which is the one case this fix targets. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `components/LensHub.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**The scroll hint became two independent, edge-aware hints, 2026-08-07, same day, exact behavior specified directly: "When I get to the bottom of the menu after scrolling it, the arrow should then go to the top to show me to scroll up. As I'm scrolling, until I get all the way to the top again, then the arrow goes back to the bottom, scrolling down shows both until I reach the bottom."** The single `canScrollMore` boolean (bottom-only fade+chevron, shown whenever any content remained below) is now two independent booleans, `canScrollDown` ("not at the bottom yet") and `canScrollUp` ("not at the top yet"), each driving its own fade+chevron pinned to its own edge of `gridWrapper` -- a top one (`scrollHintTop`, gradient from `colors.menuSurface` fading to transparent going down, `chevron-up`) alongside the existing bottom one (renamed `scrollHintBottom` for symmetry). No new state machine needed: two independent conditions naturally reproduce the exact sequence described --

- At rest, top of the list: `canScrollUp` false, `canScrollDown` true -> bottom hint only (unchanged from before).
- Scrolled into the real middle of the list (either direction): both true -> both hints show at once ("scrolling down shows both until I reach the bottom").
- Fully scrolled to the bottom: `canScrollDown` false, `canScrollUp` true -> top hint only, pointing up ("the arrow should then go to the top to show me to scroll up").
- Scrolling back up from the bottom, still short of the top: both true again -> both hints, matching the middle state.
- Back at the top: `canScrollUp` false again -> bottom hint only ("the arrow goes back to the bottom").

`recomputeScrollHint()` now derives both booleans from the same three tracked measurements (`containerHeight`/`contentHeight`/`scrollY`) on every `onLayout`/`onContentSizeChange`/`onScroll` firing -- `canScrollDown = remainingBelow > 4`, `canScrollUp = scrollY > 4`, same 4px slop on both edges so settling exactly at either end doesn't flicker. Reset to `false`/`false` on every fresh popup open (matches where a freshly-mounted `ScrollView` actually starts -- scrolled to the top -- corrected to `true`/`false` the instant the real measurements land a frame later). Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `components/LensHub.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- worth confirming the full up/down cycle for real on a category list tall enough to need it (Purple Digest, 13 categories).

**The "expanded card rises to the top of the list" behavior in Purple Digest was a real misread of the original request -- corrected 2026-08-07, same day, direct correction: "The animation to place each selected box at the top of the screen wasn't what I meant, please remove that. What I meant was when I select one, the screen scrolls it to the top so the person can read it without the need to scroll until they need to scroll. If they select a new one, the old one collapses while the new one expands and scrolls itself to the top."** The earlier 2026-08-06 fix (see that date's own entry above) had built exactly what it said at the time -- reordering the list so the tapped entry becomes the array's first item, animated via `Animated.View`'s `layout={LinearTransition}` -- but that's a different, unwanted effect (the card itself physically relocating within the list) from what was actually meant (the *screen* scrolling to bring the card, in its own unchanged position, to the top of the visible viewport).

`app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx`'s `displayEntries` reordering derivation is gone entirely -- `entries` (the plain, original category order from `getEntriesForCategory`) is rendered directly now, never reordered. In its place, a new `cardOffsets` ref (`Record<string, number>`) tracks every currently-rendered card's own real Y position within the ScrollView's content, kept live via each card's own `onLayout` (each `Animated.View` wrapper is a direct child of the ScrollView's `contentContainer`, so `nativeEvent.layout.y` already IS that card's real scroll-content offset -- no separate `measureLayout` call needed). A new `scrollEntryIntoView(id)` reads that card's offset and calls `scrollRef.current?.scrollTo({ y: offset - 12, animated: true })` -- deferred by a real animation frame (the same "wait a frame for a just-triggered layout change to land" pattern already used elsewhere in this app, e.g. `SideBuilder.tsx`'s own `onFocus`/`scrollToEnd`), with up to 5 more frames retried if the target's offset isn't known yet at all (covers `jumpToRelated` switching to a different category whose cards haven't rendered/measured for the first time). `toggleEntry` and `jumpToRelated` both now call this instead of the old flat `scrollToTop()` (which always scrolled to `y: 0` -- only correct because the old reorder logic had guaranteed the expanded card WAS the first item; with reordering gone, a flat scroll-to-zero would have been wrong for any entry that isn't already first in its category).

`Animated.View`'s own `layout={LinearTransition}` stays in place on each card wrapper -- it was never really "the bug" on its own, just misapplied to animate a reorder nobody wanted; left as-is it still does a real, wanted job: smoothly sliding the other cards in the list up/down when one card's own height changes on expand/collapse, exactly the kind of subtle reflow animation this app already uses elsewhere (SideBuilder's own field reordering, etc.).

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- this is a real behavior change from what shipped (unverified) the prior day, worth confirming the actual scroll-to-card-position feels right (timing, the 12px top margin, the cross-category jumpToRelated case) before trusting it.

**Every Purple Digest lens got a real, bespoke Info write-up, 2026-08-07, same day, explicit request: "Write the information about each digest lens for the information icon to display about it explaining that lens, as we have been doing on each of the other lenshub menus."** Every one of the 13 lenses' own `help` had just been reusing `DIGEST_CATEGORY_META`'s own one-line `description` (already shown as the on-screen category subtitle) -- fine as a picker-tile caption, too thin to stand alone as a real explanation the way Insights' own lens help write-ups already do (that file's own "Reading the table"/"Food Lookup" sections were the explicit model named).

New `DIGEST_LENS_HELP: Record<DigestCategoryKey, HelpSection>` in `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- one real, bespoke paragraph per lens (Food Additives, Problem Foods & Swaps, Gut & Microbiome, Fermented Foods, Nutrients & Micronutrients, Labs & Medication Timing, Lifestyle & Environment, Mitochondria & Metabolism, Other Autoimmune Diseases, Healing Stages, Organs & Body Systems, History & Milestones, Nutrient Interactions, Food Industry & History), each naming real, specific content from that category rather than a generic restatement -- e.g. Gut & Microbiome's own entry calls out the L-glutamine correction by name (a systematic review of 10 trials found it doesn't move the needle), Mitochondria & Metabolism names both of its own real stated tensions (fasting drives autophagy but suppresses thyroid hormone; visceral fat may partly be a defense against a leaky gut) rather than just listing topics. Typed as a `Record` (not `Partial`) over the full `DigestCategoryKey` union -- TypeScript itself now enforces that every category has a real write-up; a future category added to `DIGEST_CATEGORY_KEYS` without a matching entry here fails `tsc`, not just a silent gap.

A new shared `DIGEST_READING_HELP: HelpSection` ("Reading an entry" -- tap to expand/collapse, what the tier dot means, what a Related chip does) is appended to every lens's own bespoke section, the same shared-trailing-section pattern Insights already uses for its own `DRILLING_DOWN_HELP`, rather than repeating that same "how to use this screen" mechanic inside all 13 lenses' own text. `DIGEST_CATEGORY_META`'s own `description` field is untouched -- still the on-screen category subtitle; this is genuinely additional content, not a replacement for it. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- clean (the `Record` type itself already caught any missing key at compile time, not just a manual check). **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**Two more real fixes, 2026-08-07, same day, both reported directly: "The 'Tap the (icon) button to select a function.' message in the bottom right corner for the Digest has the wrong icon in it" and "when I scroll all the way to the bottom of the menu, the bottom arrow should disappear because I can't scroll anymore."**

1. **`PageIdentityLabel.tsx`'s own resting-prompt message never got the same Purple Digest icon fix TabHub.tsx and LensHub.tsx already have.** Both of those already special-case `route.path === '/purple-digest'` to render the real `PurpleRibbonIcon` (a traced awareness-ribbon shape) instead of the generic Ionicons "ribbon" glyph (already established elsewhere in this app as reading like a race/award rosette, not an awareness ribbon) -- but `PageIdentityLabel.tsx`'s own message was built independently and never got the same special-casing, so it kept showing the wrong, generic glyph the whole time. Fixed by adding the identical `tabRoute?.path === '/purple-digest'` check. Couldn't reuse the existing single-Text-with-inline-icon trick for this branch specifically, though: Ionicons renders as a font glyph, which nests inside a RN `Text` natively (that's exactly what let the original sentence embed an icon mid-text) -- but `PurpleRibbonIcon` is a real SVG (`react-native-svg`), which Android does not reliably lay out nested inside a `Text` the same way. The Purple Digest branch instead renders a real row of siblings (`Text`, icon, `Text`) inside a new `flexDirection: 'row', flexWrap: 'wrap'` container (`styles.textRow`) -- every other tab's message is untouched, still the original single-Text approach.
2. **The bottom scroll hint wasn't reliably disappearing once genuinely scrolled to the true bottom.** The likely real cause: `onScroll` alone is throttled (`scrollEventThrottle={100}`) -- on a fast fling that decelerates and comes to rest close to the boundary, the last throttled position update can land just short of the true settled offset, with nothing firing again once the motion actually stops to correct it. Added `onMomentumScrollEnd` (fires once, unthrottled, when a fling's deceleration finishes) and `onScrollEndDrag` (fires once when a slow drag is released directly at the boundary, no momentum phase to end) to `LensHub.tsx`'s grid `ScrollView`, both reusing the same `recomputeScrollHint()` call `onScroll` already uses -- guarantees one more accurate check fires right at the moment the scroll gesture is genuinely over, not just whatever the last throttled tick happened to catch.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on both touched files (`components/PageIdentityLabel.tsx`, `components/LensHub.tsx`) -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**A large, four-part Purple Digest expansion, 2026-08-07, same day, direct request: "There needs to be a 'Tying it all together' section in the Nutrients Interactions lens. Please check the rest of the lenses for the same necessity and create them as well. Plus, we need some what to Tie it all together of all of the digest lenses. Sort of a short story. Also, we need graph images that depicts the trends and data in ways that make it easy to understand wherever we can put them."**

1. **Per-category "Tying it all together" synthesis entries, added to 12 of the 14 categories.** Audited every category file directly (not from memory) before writing anything -- `labsMedication` already had one (`labs-timing-master-rule`, "The Practical Timing Rule, Pulled Together") and `foodIndustryHistory` already had two (`foodhistory-scapegoat-pattern`, `foodhistory-opinion-synthesis`), so those were left alone rather than duplicated. The other 12 (`foodAdditives`, `problemFoods`, `gutMicrobiome`, `fermentedFoods`, `nutrients`, `lifestyleEnvironment`, `mitochondriaMetabolism`, `otherAutoimmune`, `healingStages`, `organSystems`, `history`, `nutrientInteractions`) each got a new closing entry (`<prefix>-tying-together`) genuinely synthesizing that category's own already-established content -- not filler restating the category description, but a real throughline: e.g. Gut & Microbiome's names the intestinal barrier as the one physical structure nearly every entry in that category converges on; Mitochondria & Metabolism names both of its own real tensions (fasting vs. thyroid suppression, visceral fat as possible gut defense) as the reason the category doesn't resolve into one clean recommendation; Other Autoimmune Diseases points out the vitamin D "real correlation, unreliable trials" pattern recurring in three separate diseases. `problemFoods`' own synthesis (`problem-tying-together`) had to be shaped as a real `ProblemFoodEntry` (foodName/problem/mechanism/swaps), not a `DigestEntry` -- the type system doesn't allow mixing the two, so the synthesis content was adapted to that shape rather than bending the type.
2. **A real, mid-task correction, caught by the user directly**: "Remove all entries you created that say thing such as 'This entry is the map for the rest of this category -- read it first.'" That exact phrase was in a PRE-EXISTING entry (`healing-stage-map`, from the earlier same-day Healing Stages port), not something written in this pass -- grepped the whole `lib/digest/` directory for the same class of meta, self-referential "read this first"/"start here" navigation language and found two more instances (`healing-stage-map`'s own teaser, `otherAutoimmune`'s `other-why-cross-disease-evidence` teaser). All three fixed by removing the meta commentary while keeping the entry's own real content -- and the new synthesis entries written afterward were deliberately written without this pattern (relationships between entries are expressed via `relatedIds` cross-links instead of "read X first" instructions).
3. **A new "The Big Picture" 15th category (`lib/digest/bigPicture.ts`), the requested "short story."** Genuinely different in kind from the 12 per-category syntheses above: 7 short chapters following one illustrative, explicitly-labeled-as-fictional day (morning dose -> breakfast -> midday -> afternoon -> evening -> the longer historical arc -> what it's all for), touching all 14 other categories in a single continuous read rather than reviewing them one at a time. Every factual claim woven into the narrative is real and either cited directly or cross-linked via `relatedIds` to its own fully-cited entry elsewhere -- only the day/person themselves are a storytelling device, stated as such in the file's own header comment, never presented as a real logged case. Wired in the same way every prior category addition was: `DIGEST_CATEGORY_KEYS` (`types.ts`), `DIGEST_CATEGORY_META` (`index.ts`, icon `book-outline`, placed last per this file's own established "append, don't reorder" practice), and a new required `DIGEST_LENS_HELP.bigPicture` entry in `purple-digest.tsx` (the `Record<DigestCategoryKey, HelpSection>` type caught the omission at compile time before it could ship incomplete, the same safety net built in 2026-08-06).
4. **Real chart images, not decorative ones.** New `DigestChart`/`DigestChartDatum` types (`lib/digest/types.ts`) and a new `components/DigestChart.tsx` (`DigestBarChart`) -- a plain, horizontal bar chart built from styled `View`s, not `react-native-svg` or a charting library (React Native has no built-in charting; a bar is just a `View` with a proportional width, and horizontal bars were chosen specifically because this app's own real datasets carry long labels like "2001-02" or a full disease name that fit a row's own left-hand text far better than a cramped vertical bar on a phone screen). `chart` is optional on both `DigestEntry` and `ProblemFoodEntry`, and deliberately NOT added everywhere -- only where an entry's own summary already states a small set of real, comparable numbers, reusing those exact figures rather than inventing new ones (stated as a hard rule in `DigestChart`'s own type comment). Added to 7 real entries across 5 categories: `foodhistory-timeline-modern-surge` (ultra-processed food's rising share of calories), `foodhistory-soil-real-depletion` (zinc/copper loss under tillage), `organ-kidney` (eGFR by thyroid status), `gut-zonulin-timeline` (real trial timelines), `interaction-tannins-iron` and `interaction-phytates-minerals` (both Nutrient Interactions), and `problem-coffee-timing`. Wired into `DigestCard`'s render in `purple-digest.tsx` for both entry shapes -- `DigestEntry`'s own tier color drives the bar color there; `ProblemFoodEntry` has no tier concept, so its chart uses a fixed `colors.accent`.

A final, whole-directory verification pass (a throwaway Node script, not just `tsc`) confirmed zero dangling `relatedIds` across all 192 entries now in the Digest -- caught and fixed one genuinely pre-existing dangling reference along the way (`gut-blautia-lupus-zonulin`'s own `relatedIds` pointed at `other-lupus-probiotic-rct`, which was never a real id -- the actual entry is `other-lupus`), not introduced by this pass but found by the same verification sweep. `PURPLE_DIGEST_VERSION` bumped. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` across every touched/new file -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- this is a large addition (19 new entries, a new category, a new rendering component, 7 new charts) sight-unseen on a real device; worth a real walkthrough before trusting the chart layout/readability specifically.

**Two more real fixes, 2026-08-07, same day, both reported directly.**

1. **A genuine scroll/expand bug in the entry list itself, not just an unwanted side effect of the earlier "scroll to top on expand" feature.** Reported precisely: reading "Why This Category Exists At All," scrolling down just a little to see "Rheumatoid Arthritis," tapping it -- and the screen forcibly jumped that card all the way to the exact top while the previous card collapsed at the same instant, even though the tapped card was already comfortably visible. Root cause: `scrollEntryIntoView()` (`app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx`) unconditionally scrolled to the target card's exact computed position on every expand, with no check for whether the card was already close enough to the top to not need moving at all -- combined with the previous card's own collapse animation shifting layout at the same moment, two things moving the screen at once read as genuinely wrong, not just unnecessary. Fixed with a new `SCROLL_SKIP_THRESHOLD` (80px, roughly one collapsed card's own header height): the function now tracks the ScrollView's own live scroll position (`currentScrollY`, kept current via a new `onScroll` handler on the main `ScrollView`) and skips the scroll entirely when the computed target is already within that threshold of the current position. A card genuinely deep in a long list -- the actual case this whole mechanism exists for -- still scrolls for real; a card that's already basically visible just expands in place, no forced jump.
2. **The Digest entries were reported as genuinely under-written, not just a UI nitpick**: "One short paragraph with several thoughts isn't what I was going for. I want each one to have a beginning middle and end like any story, and it needs to be interesting to read and make the person want to read more." Given the scale of the ask (192 entries across 14 categories), rewrote one full category first as a concrete demonstration rather than guessing at a new voice across everything at once -- `otherAutoimmune.ts` (9 entries), chosen because it's the exact category named in the report and because its own "looks unrelated to Hashimoto's on the surface" shape lends itself naturally to a real hook-and-payoff structure. Every entry now opens on a genuine hook (usually the surface-level reason the disease looks unrelated to Hashimoto's at all -- "Rheumatoid arthritis has nothing to do with the thyroid gland itself..."), develops the real finding in a real middle section, and closes on why it actually matters, rather than stating the finding, the mechanism, and the caveat as three flat, back-to-back clauses in one paragraph the old version used throughout. Every underlying fact, number, and citation is unchanged from the original pass -- this is a real prose rewrite for narrative pull, not new research. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on both touched files -- clean; re-ran the whole-directory dangling-`relatedIds` check afterward too (still zero, all 192 ids intact). **This category is meant as the template, not the finish line -- the other 13 categories' entries have NOT been rewritten yet and still read in the older, denser style.** Next step (not yet done, explicitly flagged rather than assumed): confirm this new voice actually lands before spending the very large amount of remaining effort applying it to the other ~183 entries.

**The scroll fix above was a real misread, corrected the same day, direct correction: "The scroll is still not doing what I asked for the way it is supposed to work. No matter which box I choose, no matter where it is at that moment on the screen, it's header moves to the top of the screen and it's information opens below it. The header of the one I tapped should be at the top of the screen under the app's own header section buy about 10 pixels."** The `SCROLL_SKIP_THRESHOLD` fix from earlier the same day (skip the forced scroll when a card is already close to the top) had guessed wrong about what the original report actually meant -- the real spec, now stated precisely, is that EVERY tap scrolls, unconditionally, regardless of where the card already sits on screen. Reverted the skip logic entirely (and the now-unused `currentScrollY` tracking/`onScroll` handler that existed only to support it) back to always calling `scrollTo`. `ENTRY_SCROLL_TOP_MARGIN` set to the exact figure given, 10 (was 12). Confirmed this also correctly means the very first entry in a category (sitting directly under the category's own `headerCard` description box) still scrolls on tap, pushing that description box off-screen so the tapped entry's own header lands at the true top -- matching "no matter which box I choose, no matter where it is" literally, not just for entries deep in a long list. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- this specific behavior has now been guessed at twice; worth confirming directly rather than assuming a third guess landed right.

**A real, third scroll bug found and fixed the same day, direct report: works correctly tapping entries in order top to bottom, but "if I scroll down past the next one and select it instead the app scrolls that category off of the top of the screen so the very bottom of the chosen category is barely showing at the top."** The margin/unconditional-scroll fix above was correct as far as it went but didn't explain this specific overshoot. Root cause, reasoned through rather than guessed blind a third time: the distinguishing factor between "taps in order" (worked) and "scroll further down, then tap something farther away" (overshot) isn't tap order, it's scroll momentum -- a bigger manual scroll gesture leaves Android's native ScrollView still decelerating (mid-fling) at the moment of the tap, and calling `scrollTo` while a fling is still running is a real, documented source of an inconsistent final resting position on Android specifically (the new programmatic scroll can compound with the still-running momentum instead of cleanly replacing it). Fixed with the standard workaround: re-added live scroll-position tracking (`currentScrollY`, via the main `ScrollView`'s own `onScroll`, `scrollEventThrottle={16}` this time -- tighter than the 100 used elsewhere, since this needs an up-to-date position to halt against, not just a coarse hint), and `scrollEntryIntoView()` now issues an immediate, unanimated `scrollTo({ y: currentScrollY.current, animated: false })` first -- which has nothing to visually animate but does cancel any in-flight native fling -- immediately followed by the real animated scroll to the actual target. The animated scroll now always starts from a known-stopped state, never a still-moving one. Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- this exact interaction has now had three real attempts; the next report on this specific behavior should get a careful, on-device-informed look rather than a fourth blind guess if it's still not right.

**The scroll bug's real mechanism was fundamentally wrong, not just mistuned -- rebuilt from scratch 2026-08-07, same day, after a direct, pointed correction: "You seem to be trying to judge their approximate location and approximate destination at the time instead of just assigning something to be the mechanism for each box... place the header of this box 10 pixels from the bottom of the header of the app."** That's exactly right, and it names the actual root cause behind all three prior attempts at this same bug: every earlier version tried to track and cache each card's own Y position ahead of time (via `onLayout`, updated asynchronously, read later) and then guess, via `requestAnimationFrame` retries, whether that cached number had caught up with reality yet -- the same class of problem a web page's own anchor-link navigation never has, because it doesn't cache anything; it just asks the browser "where is this element right now" at the moment it's needed.

`app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` now does the direct equivalent: `cardOffsets` (the old cached-Y-position ref) is gone entirely, replaced by `cardRefs` -- a real ref to each rendered card. `scrollEntryIntoView()` calls `.measure()` (a real, standard React Native primitive both `Animated.View` and `ScrollView` expose, reporting a view's own true, current on-screen position, queried fresh every single call) on both the target card and the ScrollView itself, computes the real absolute scroll target from those two live measurements (`currentScrollY.current + (cardPageY - scrollPageY) - ENTRY_SCROLL_TOP_MARGIN`), and scrolls there -- never a value computed and cached at some earlier, possibly-stale moment. A new `Measurable` structural type (just the one `.measure()` method signature both ref types actually need) avoids requiring an exact, brittle component type for either ref. The one real animation frame of deferral that remains is no longer "wait for a cached offset to become available" -- there's no cache left to wait on -- it's "let a just-triggered sibling collapse actually land first," since `.measure()` needs the real, POST-collapse layout to already be committed to report the right number; the retry loop is now purely for "the target card hasn't mounted yet at all" (e.g. `jumpToRelated` switching categories), not for a measurement coming back wrong. The momentum-halt step from the previous fix (an immediate unanimated scroll to the current position before the real animated one, to cancel any in-flight Android fling) is kept unchanged, since that was solving a genuinely different problem than this one.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- this is the fourth real attempt at this specific bug, this time built on live measurement rather than cached tracking, which is a structurally different (and more defensible) approach than the previous three, not just another parameter tweak -- still worth confirming directly rather than assumed fixed.

Both new citations independently verified via `WebFetch` against real PubMed pages (this session's own `WebSearch` budget was already exhausted, confirmed by a direct test call), not fabricated. of autoimmune thyroid disease patients had low B12, 31% of those had real pernicious anemia, from a 2006 study), magnesium (with an honest, well-documented limitation stated up front: serum magnesium reflects under 1% of total body stores), zinc & copper (real documented cases of zinc supplementation causing copper-deficiency anemia and neurological symptoms), selenium (honestly flagged as the reduces LDL/total cholesterol in subclinical hypothyroidism), A1c/fasting glucose, fasting insulin (honestly tiered weak -- real appeal, genuinely less standardized than glucose/A1c), hs-CRP, sex hormones/menopause panel (**a second real correction to the shared summary** -- a real 2003 JAMA review found perimenopause is usually a clinical, symptom-based diagnosis, not one hormone levels reliably confirm, since estradiol/FSH fluctuate too much cycle to cycle), and cortisol testing (drawing a real, sourced line between legitimate testing for suspected adrenal insufficiency/APS-2 and the wellness-industry "adrenal fatigue" framing a 2025 review states plainly "are not part of the medical or endocrine lexicon").

**The scroll-to-top bug's real, fifth root cause found and fixed, 2026-08-07, same day, direct report: "some of the time when I choose a box now it will not align the box top just under the app header bottom edge."** The 4th fix (live `.measure()` instead of cached offsets) was correct in principle but still missed a real, different problem: it only waited one animation frame (~16ms) before measuring, nowhere near enough time for the LinearTransition animation collapsing whatever card was previously open to actually finish sliding every card into its real, final position. Measuring that early caught the target card still mid-slide -- not a measurement-mechanism bug this time, a pure timing race against an animation that takes far longer than one frame. This exactly explains "some of the time": tapping the very first card of a session, with nothing previously open to collapse, has nothing to wait for and was never actually broken; tapping any card after that -- the common case, since this is a single-open accordion -- always requires something else to collapse first, and that's exactly when the race showed up.

Fixed by removing the guesswork entirely: `LinearTransition` on each card's `Animated.View` now carries an explicit, pinned duration (`LinearTransition.duration(CARD_LAYOUT_TRANSITION_MS)`, 300ms) instead of Reanimated's own implicit default, and `scrollEntryIntoView` now waits that exact real number (plus a small buffer for ordinary JS-thread scheduling slack, `CARD_LAYOUT_SETTLE_BUFFER_MS`, 60ms) via a real `setTimeout` before ever calling `.measure()` -- not a guess at "probably long enough," the literal duration the same animation is configured to take. The existing one-frame `requestAnimationFrame` retry loop is unchanged and still runs first, but now only for its own original, narrower job (confirming the target card's ref exists at all yet, e.g. after `jumpToRelated` switches to a category whose cards haven't mounted for the first time) -- the real animation-settle wait begins only once that's confirmed. A deliberate, honest tradeoff stated plainly rather than hidden: every tap now waits a real, consistent ~360ms before the scroll itself animates, even in the rare case nothing actually needed to collapse -- correctness was the explicit priority over shaving that delay for an edge case.

Verified via `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` on `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` -- clean. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- this is the fifth real attempt at this specific bug, and the first one built around an actual identified root cause (a timing race against a known, named animation) rather than another guess at the measurement mechanism itself -- still worth confirming directly, including that the new, consistent ~360ms pause before each scroll doesn't itself read as sluggish.

**The Digest was confirmed working, then the person asked directly what real research gaps remained -- and, on confirming agreement with the answer, asked for all of it built out at once ("I want you to begin on all of it in whatever order you prefer to do them"), 2026-08-07, same day.** A real, substantial expansion followed: **21 new entries across 8 research clusters**, spanning one new category (Pregnancy & Family Planning) and additions to six existing ones (Labs & Medication Timing, Organs & Body Systems, Nutrients & Micronutrients, Self Advocacy, Lifestyle & Environment, History & Milestones, Problem Foods & Swaps). This session's own `WebSearch` budget was already exhausted (confirmed again by a direct test call at the start of this pass), so every single citation below was found the same established `WebFetch`-against-real-PubMed-pages fallback already used repeatedly this session -- real papers read directly, several guessed PMIDs along the way caught as WRONG by direct verification and corrected rather than shipped (a real, recurring discipline check, not just a one-time catch).

**Labs & Medication Timing, 4 new entries.** `labs-combination-t3-ndt` tackles probably the single most common real question in Hashimoto's patient communities -- should I ask for T3 added to my levothyroxine, or switch to natural desiccated thyroid -- with a genuinely honest, two-sided answer: a real 2006 meta-analysis (11 RCTs, 1,216 patients) and a real 2013 crossover RCT (70 patients, DTE vs. LT4) both found no measurable group-level symptom/QoL difference, YET a real 2025 systematic review (11 RCTs, 1,135 patients) found 52% of patients still preferred combination therapy versus 24% for LT4 alone -- a real, unresolved gap between what a trial measures at the group level and what an individual actually experiences, presented as exactly that tension rather than resolved either way. `labs-drug-induced-thyroid-dysfunction` covers amiodarone (real ETA 2018 guidelines put dysfunction at 15-20% of patients, from a real iodine-content/structural-similarity mechanism) and lithium (~8% real rate of induced goiter/hypothyroidism, via a real deiodination-slowing mechanism plus direct immune stimulation specifically in antibody-positive patients -- a real, direct Hashimoto's-relevant detail). `labs-checkpoint-inhibitor-thyroiditis` covers cancer immunotherapy's real, documented thyroiditis risk, with the genuinely Hashimoto's-relevant finding that real research found the risk measurably higher specifically in patients already antibody-positive before treatment starts. `labs-absorption-interferers-beyond-food` extends the existing calcium/iron/coffee timing rules to real medications (PPIs, cholestyramine, phosphate binders, ciprofloxacin) via a real, comprehensive 2017 review naming the interference "established and clinically significant" across that whole list.

**Organs & Body Systems, 2 new entries.** `organ-overtreatment-bone-heart-risk` covers the real, less-discussed flip side of every under-treatment risk already in this category: a real classic 1994 NEJM study (Sawin et al., 10-year follow-up) found a 3.1x higher atrial fibrillation risk with low TSH, and a real 2001 Annals of Internal Medicine study (Bauer et al., Study of Osteoporotic Fractures) found a 3.6x higher hip fracture risk and 4.5x higher vertebral fracture risk -- real, named, quantified over-treatment risks, framed as a direct argument for the periodic retesting Self Advocacy already recommends, not a reason to fear levothyroxine. `organ-primary-thyroid-lymphoma` covers a real, rare (roughly 0.5% transformation rate, under 5% of all thyroid cancers) but genuinely important complication, with the one real, actionable warning sign named directly: a goiter that starts enlarging rapidly, unlike Hashimoto's own typically slow, gradual course.

**Pregnancy & Family Planning -- a real, new 17th Digest category, 5 entries.** A genuine, previously-total gap: Organs & Body Systems already covered real miscarriage-risk research, but nothing anywhere covered trimester-specific TSH targets, postpartum thyroiditis as its own distinct entity, breastfeeding safety, or pregnancy's own real, increased iodine stakes. `pregnancy-tsh-target` covers the real, lower TSH target (below 2.5 mIU/L) once pregnancy starts or is being planned, citing the real 2017 ATA pregnancy-specific guidelines. `pregnancy-postpartum-thyroiditis` is genuinely the most striking real finding in this whole batch: a real prospective study of over 4,000 women found postpartum thyroiditis in 3.9% of pregnancies (11.1% in already-high-risk women), 82% of affected women had a real hypothyroid phase within the first postpartum year, and -- directly challenging the older, more reassuring assumption -- 54% still had persistent hypothyroidism at the one-year mark, not the temporary blip it's often assumed to be. `pregnancy-breastfeeding-safety` gives a real, reassuring answer via the NIH's own LactMed database: levothyroxine is itself a normal component of human breast milk, and real thyroid-society guidance actively recommends treating hypothyroidism while nursing, not pausing it. `pregnancy-iodine-needs` extends the existing two-edged-iodine framing to pregnancy's own real, higher stakes on both the deficiency and excess side. A real closing `pregnancy-tying-together` entry pulls the four together around one practical thread: this is a genuine, time-limited window where the standard 6-12-month testing cadence is deliberately too infrequent.

**Nutrients & Micronutrients, 2 new entries.** `nutrient-ashwagandha` covers the herb actually most discussed in real Hashimoto's patient communities (more than Nigella sativa, which already had its own entry) -- a real double-blind RCT (50 subclinical hypothyroid patients, 46 completers, 8 weeks, 600mg/day) found significant TSH/T3/T4 improvement versus placebo, with an honest, real caution named directly: as a genuine thyroid-hormone-raising herb, it carries a real, mechanistic reason for caution in anyone hyperthyroid or being treated for Graves'. `nutrient-iodine-supplement-caution` extends the existing kelp warning to a genuinely different, easy-to-overlook exposure route -- standalone "thyroid support" iodine pills, often marketed directly to the exact population most at risk of the same real excess-iodine-triggers-autoimmune-flare mechanism. The category's own `nutrient-tying-together` synthesis was updated to reflect both new entries and the real, honest ranking they land in (middle tier, alongside Nigella sativa -- fewer, smaller trials than selenium's own much deeper evidence base).

**Self Advocacy, 2 new entries.** `advocacy-tsh-optimal-range-debate` covers a real, named, ongoing disagreement within endocrinology itself -- a real 2005 JCEM paper (Wartofsky & Dickey) argued the standard TSH reference range is too wide (skewed by an original reference population that likely included undiagnosed thyroid disease), with a real companion paper the same year explicitly proposing a narrower 0.4-2.5 mIU/L "optimal" range -- presented honestly as a genuine, unresolved disagreement the standard range hasn't been displaced by, not a settled case either way. `advocacy-seronegative-hashimotos` covers a real, meaningful minority (12.3% in one real pediatric cohort study) of clinically/ultrasound-confirmed Hashimoto's cases that never test antibody-positive at all, with a real adult study finding these cases tend toward a milder clinical picture -- a real, useful fact so a negative antibody result doesn't get treated as ruling out an otherwise well-supported diagnosis.

**Lifestyle & Environment, 3 new entries.** `lifestyle-ebv-viral-trigger` is a real correction of this app's own earlier, unverified claim (a stale "11.1% of Hashimoto's thyroid tissue" figure this document itself had carried since 2026-08-05, never actually converted into a real Digest entry, and never independently re-verified until this pass) -- real direct verification of the actual underlying study (Janegova et al. 2015) found EBV genetic evidence in 80.7% of Hashimoto's thyroid tissue samples via one marker (EBER) and 34.5% via a more specific active-infection marker (LMP1), versus 0% in Graves' tissue from the same study -- corrected to the real, verified numbers rather than repeating the unverified figure, with the real proposed mechanism (direct inflammatory signaling from active thyroid-cell infection) stated accurately rather than forced into the molecular-mimicry framing that mechanism doesn't actually claim. `lifestyle-covid19-thyroid-trigger` covers a real systematic review finding 20 documented cases of new/reactivated autoimmune thyroid disease following COVID-19 (14 Graves', 5 Hashimoto's, 1 postpartum), most reaching remission within 3 months, with two real, severe fatal cases (thyroid storm, myxedema coma) underscoring the review's own real, practical recommendation to check thyroid function during and after a serious COVID-19 infection. `lifestyle-sleep-apnea` is deliberately tiered weak -- a real, recent 2025 review confirms genuine epidemiological associations between sleep apnea and thyroid disorders but states directly, in its own words, that "discrepancies and contradictory results remain" and the mechanism "is not clear," presented honestly rather than dressed up with an invented, tidier-sounding explanation this app couldn't actually verify.

**History & Milestones, 2 new entries.** `history-whickham-progression-rate` traces a real, common anxious question ("my antibodies are positive but TSH is still normal -- how worried should I be?") back to its actual real answer: the real, landmark 20-year Whickham Survey follow-up (2,779 adults) found women with BOTH elevated TSH and positive antibodies had 38 times higher odds of developing overt hypothyroidism than women with neither -- a real, quantified number directly supporting Self Advocacy's own periodic-retesting recommendation. `history-heritability-family-risk` adds a genuinely different, complementary kind of evidence to the category's existing GWAS/CTLA-4 entry -- real twin-study heritability data (a 110,814-twin population study) found heritability running from 0.60 (Graves') to 0.97 (Addison's) across organ-specific autoimmune diseases, honestly caveated that this app couldn't independently verify Hashimoto's own specific figure within that range this session -- plus real, additional named susceptibility genes (HLA-DR variants, PTPN22) beyond CTLA-4, closing on a real, practical family-risk point this document hadn't stated plainly before.

**Problem Foods & Swaps, 1 new entry.** `problem-gluten-free-without-celiac` tackles probably the single most common Hashimoto's diet question with a genuinely surprising, honest answer: a real, very recent (2025) systematic review and meta-analysis (3 RCTs, 110 participants) found going gluten-free actually significantly INCREASED anti-TPO antibodies while significantly DECREASING anti-Tg antibodies -- moving in opposite directions on the app's own two core antibody markers -- with no significant TSH/FT3/FT4 change either way, and the review's own authors rating the underlying evidence as having "serious methodological concerns" and being "very uncertain." Deliberately distinguished from the real, separate, already-covered gut-permeability mechanism (gliadin/zonulin, applicable regardless of celiac status) and from genuine celiac disease, where gluten-free eating remains medically necessary independent of any thyroid-specific effect.

Full-directory verification after all 21 entries: **293 total unique ids across the whole Digest, zero duplicates, zero dangling `relatedIds`.** `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` clean across every touched/new file (one real syntax error caught mid-pass: a literal, unescaped `"` character inside a double-quoted string, caught by `tsc` itself on first draft and fixed immediately -- the same class of quoting bug this whole Digest build has hit and fixed several times before). `PURPLE_DIGEST_VERSION` bumped. Purple Digest now has **17 real categories plus Problem Foods & Swaps**, 293 total entries. **Not yet exercised on-device** -- same standing caveat as the rest of this whole day's work; this is the single largest content addition since the original 16-category build, entirely unseen on a real device.

**Two more real, direct requests the same day, both explicitly framed with real uncertainty by the person themselves -- "I'm not sure, this sounds like maybe it could go down a rabbit hole quickly" for the first, no such caveat for the second.** (1) Real research into chiropractic care, acupuncture/acupressure, massage, and heat/cold therapy's actual correlation to Hashimoto's or symptom relief, explicitly asking whether it should tie into exercise. (2) A real reference to the "prescribing cascade" phenomenon -- one medication leading to another when an underlying cause (inflammation) goes unaddressed -- and real evidence that medications for cholesterol, blood pressure, or fibromyalgia can sometimes become unnecessary as underlying health improves.

**Complementary & Manual Therapies -- a new, 18th Digest category, 7 entries, deliberately bounded per the person's own stated rabbit-hole concern.** Six real, named modalities researched directly, each reported honestly regardless of what was actually found -- this pass did NOT pad thin evidence into false confidence. `complementary-chiropractic` reports a real, flat null: a direct search for any study connecting chiropractic care to thyroid or autoimmune disease returned zero results, stated plainly as "never studied," not "studied and found ineffective." `complementary-acupuncture` found a real, well-designed 2021 trial protocol registered specifically for Hashimoto's thyroiditis, with no published results locatable -- reported honestly as "pending," not padded with general acupuncture research standing in for it. `complementary-acupressure` covers real, borrowed fatigue-relief evidence from other chronic illnesses (leukemia, chronic fatigue syndrome, hemodialysis patients), explicitly labeled as not Hashimoto's-specific, the same "borrow honestly, label clearly" discipline already established for Other Autoimmune Diseases. `complementary-massage-cortisol` reports a real, genuinely surprising finding: a comprehensive quantitative review found the existing research on massage lowering cortisol simply "are not in agreement" -- one of the most commonly repeated wellness claims anywhere turns out less settled than its popularity suggests. `complementary-heat-sauna` is the real standout of the category: a real, large Finnish cohort study found 4-7x/week sauna use tied to a 63% lower sudden-cardiac-death risk versus once weekly, with an honest caveat about how far one specific cultural cohort's data generalizes, plus a real, more complicated short-term finding (IL-6 actually rising acutely alongside anti-inflammatory IL-1RA, not a simple inflammation-lowering effect). `complementary-cold-exposure` covers a real, striking PNAS study (Kox et al. 2014) with an honest correction of what it actually tested -- a combined meditation/breathing/cold-immersion protocol measurably blunting real inflammatory response to a deliberate endotoxin challenge, explicitly NOT proof that cold exposure alone does the same thing. A closing `complementary-tying-together` entry directly answers the person's own "should this tie into exercise" question rather than leaving it open: yes, and exercise (already covered in Mitochondria & Metabolism) remains the better-evidenced, more directly applicable choice of everything across both categories -- these six modalities are honestly-tiered complements, not substitutes.

**The prescribing-cascade thread, 2 new entries in Self Advocacy.** `advocacy-prescribing-cascade` names the real, established clinical-pharmacology concept directly -- a real, authoritative 2019 definition (McCarthy, Visentin, Rochon, tracing back to a real classic 1997 BMJ paper) states it plainly: a drug's own side effect misinterpreted as a new medical condition, treated with a second drug. The real, hopeful flip side is backed by real trial data, not just intuition: the real DiRECT trial found 28% of people who achieved diabetes remission through a structured weight-loss intervention (mean 11.4kg loss) remained completely off blood pressure medication at 24 months -- a real, quantified, honestly partial success rate, explicitly framed as a reason to ask a doctor directly whether a medication is still needed, never a case for stopping anything without supervision. `advocacy-fibromyalgia-thyroid-overlap` covers a real, documented overlap between fibromyalgia and Hashimoto's beyond shared symptoms -- multiple real studies found elevated thyroid autoimmunity in fibromyalgia patients, and one real, specific, striking finding: 34% (33 of 103) of fibromyalgia patients in one real study were being treated with thyroid hormone, a rate high enough the study's own title calls it "excess use" -- a real, direct, practical reason a fibromyalgia diagnosis is worth pairing with the full thyroid panel this app's own Self Advocacy category already recommends, rather than either diagnosis fully explaining away symptoms that might belong to the other.

Full-directory verification after all 9 new entries: **302 total unique ids across the whole Digest, zero duplicates, zero dangling `relatedIds`.** `npx tsc --noEmit`/`npx eslint` clean across every touched/new file. `PURPLE_DIGEST_VERSION` bumped. Purple Digest now has **18 real categories plus Problem Foods & Swaps**, 302 total entries. **Not yet exercised on-device.**

**Every one of the 19 `lib/digest/*.ts` content files rewritten to remove AI-writing tells, 2026-08-08, direct, blunt feedback: "I hate to say it, but you'll need to go through and unAI all of the data in Digest. There are a lot of tells that AI wrote it rather than a person, such as saying something like 'it's not just this, but its that and that's worth looking at'. There are the dashes AI uses instead of punctuation. Please rewrite things so it doesn't appear that AI wrote it."** Confirmed the scale before touching anything, not just the two patterns named directly: `grep` across every `lib/digest/*.ts` file found "--" used as punctuation 1,383 times, "real" 1,596 times, "genuinely" 266 times, "worth" 166 times, the "honest(ly)" family 123 times, and "not just" 83 times, a severe, file-wide pattern, not a handful of stray sentences.

Scoped deliberately to user-facing content fields only: `title`, `teaser`, `summary` (or `foodName`/`problem`/`mechanism`/`swaps` for the differently-shaped `ProblemFoodEntry` in `problemFoods.ts`), and `citations[].source` strings, across all 19 files, plus the `description` fields in `DIGEST_CATEGORY_META` (`index.ts`, the picker subtitles) and the `DIGEST_HELP_SECTIONS`/`DIGEST_READING_HELP`/`DIGEST_LENS_HELP` blocks in `app/(tabs)/purple-digest.tsx` (the Info-sheet write-ups).
